# Chapter 1

[T]{.dropcap}he binder had a tab for rain.

It had a tab for the string quartet, a tab for the shuttle schedule, a tab for the allergy list, which had grown over the summer from three names to eleven, because apparently half of Toronto had developed a relationship with gluten that required documentation. It had a tab labeled SUNDAY in Carol’s own block capitals, though Sunday was only brunch, and brunch, as she had told Sylvie twice, would run itself if the coffee urns arrived by nine.

Carol stood at the kitchen island at seven in the morning with the binder open to VENDORS and the phone pinned between her ear and shoulder, on hold with the rental company, listening to a flute arrangement of a song she was fairly sure had been popular when Maren was in diapers. Through the window over the sink the light was coming up gold and slantwise through the maple, which had started turning at the crown, a week early. The forecast said twenty-four degrees by afternoon. Harvest weather. Somewhere east of town, along the Parkway, they would already be picking.

Biscuit lay across the heating vent by the stove, twelve years old and committed to it, thumping his tail once when Glen came down in his golf shirt with his reading glasses pushed up into his hair.

“You’re on hold,” Glen said. “It’s seven.”

“They open at seven.”

“They open their phone line at seven. There’s a man named Dave standing in a warehouse right now watching that phone ring and finishing his coffee, and there’s nothing you can do about Dave.”

“I can outlast Dave.”

“That I believe.” He kissed the top of her head on his way to the coffee maker and stood looking into the cupboard as if the mugs might have rearranged themselves overnight. He took the blue one, the one with the chip he refused to retire, and filled it, and leaned against the counter to watch her the way he watched her every time the household went into what he called tournament conditions. Amused. Proprietary, and she had decided long ago to find it flattering.

The flute cut off. A human voice replaced it, and Carol straightened.

“Good morning, this is Carol Hastings, order 4471, the Corran Estate wedding this Saturday. I’m calling about the chair count.” A pause. “Yes. The confirmation you sent lists a hundred and forty. The contract says a hundred and sixty-two.” Another pause, longer. She pulled the contract from the tab where it lived and read out the line, the date, the initials in the margin, hers and Dave’s, as it happened. She did not raise her voice. She never had to anymore; she had spent thirty years perfecting a tone that made men in warehouses reach for their own paperwork, and she deployed it now, pleasantly, like a hand laid flat on a table.

By the time she hung up, the count was a hundred and sixty-two, delivery moved from Friday afternoon to Friday morning, and Dave had apologized twice.

“Poor Dave,” said Glen.

“Dave is fine. Dave gets to be a story Dave tells.” She made a note in the margin, closed the binder, and let herself have one mouthful of coffee, which had gone lukewarm on the island and was still, somehow, the best thing that had happened all morning. “Marta’s at eight thirty, Sylvie’s at ten, your sister gets here whenever your sister gets here, and Trish said noon, which means two.”

“Which means she’ll be here at eleven to prove you wrong.”

“That would be a first.”

“She’ll do it just for you. Special wedding pricing.” He drained the blue mug and set it in the sink, and then, on the way past, he did the thing he did, laying his palm flat between her shoulder blades, warm through her shirt, two seconds, the physical punctuation he had used for thirty years to mean here I am, and there you are, and off I go. “I’m at the shop till two. If Peterson calls about the arena bid, I’ve moved it to Monday. Everything till Monday is moved to Monday.”

“Except the wedding.”

“The wedding,” said Glen, “is the arena bid,” and he went out through the mudroom whistling, and the screen door clapped shut behind him, and Biscuit lifted his head at the sound and put it back down.

The house did not stay quiet for long. It had been filling all week, the way a tide comes in, imperceptibly and then all at once. Maren and Ben had driven down from Toronto on Sunday and were staying at Ben’s parents’ rental in Old Town, an arrangement Carol had privately audited for etiquette and approved. Glen’s cousin’s family had taken over the Pillar and Post. There were relatives in the guest room, or there would be by tonight, Lorraine and Roger, arriving from Burlington with what Lorraine had promised on the phone would be just a few little things for the welcome bags, which Carol had learned over three decades meant a trunk.

She worked through the morning in the pocket of quiet before all of it. The seating chart was printed and taped to the dining room wall, a hundred and sixty-two names on tented cards she had ordered in a serif that matched the invitations, because Maren had asked for one weekend where nothing clashed, and Carol had taken the request as a personal commission. She stood in front of the chart with her second coffee and read it the way other people read a crossword. Table nine still had the empty seat where the Hendersons’ son had dropped out of the count. Table four had two exes at safe diagonal. Table one had the parents, and she stood looking at her own name on its little tented card, CAROL, next to GLEN, next to LORRAINE & ROGER, and thought that there were worse summaries of a life.

At eight thirty-one, Marta called about the flowers, and it went the way calls with Marta went, which was to say beautifully and at great length. The dahlias out of Vineland were, in Marta’s words, showing off. The eucalyptus was a hostage situation, do not ask. The delivery to the house Saturday morning would come between eight and eight fifteen, personal bouquets and pin-ons in one labeled box, and Carol wrote 8:00 MARTA, PERSONAL FLOWERS, ONE BOX in the SATURDAY tab in block capitals while Marta told her a story about a groom in Beamsville who had eaten a boutonniere on a dare.

“Ate it.”

“The rose. Petals and all. The best man filmed it.”

“And the marriage?”

“Thriving, from what I hear. You can’t tell from the outside, Carol, that’s what I always say. Some of them eat the flowers and live happily ever after.” Marta was still laughing when she hung up, and Carol stood in her kitchen smiling at the SATURDAY tab and thinking she might miss this part when it was over, the daily company of competent women on the phone.

Sylvie at ten was crisper, a shared screen and a run sheet, the day broken into minutes. One forty-five, doors. Two o’clock, processional. Two twenty-five, signing table. Two thirty, receiving line on the terrace, and here Sylvie paused and said what she’d said before, which was that plenty of couples skipped the line these days, it ate half an hour and it was murder in heels.

“Maren wants it,” Carol said. “She wants to see every person’s face. Her words.”

“Then we build in the half hour, and I’ll have flats stashed behind the arbor for whoever needs them.” A tap of keys. “You’re my easiest mother of the season, you know that? I had one in July who wanted approval rights on the officiant’s shoes.”

“Give me time,” said Carol. “It’s only Tuesday.”

The morning left her one clear hour, and she spent it on foot. The printer on Queen Street had the welcome-bag maps ready, a hundred of them, Old Town drawn in a wandering hand-inked style Maren had picked because it looked like somewhere you’d want to get lost, and Carol collected them in their paper band and added a spool of navy ribbon from the stationer two doors down, because the ribbon Lorraine was bringing would be wrong. It would be lovely, and it would be wrong, and having the right ribbon already in the house would save everyone a conversation.

Queen Street in the third week of September ran at half its summer speed. The tour buses had thinned to weekenders; the hanging baskets were on their last extravagant legs; outside the bakery the chalkboard had switched from lemonade to cider. She was reading it, deciding against, when Susan Pell came out of the bakery with a box of butter tarts and lit up like a porch light.

“Carol Hastings. The woman of the hour.”

“Susan.”

“Four days. Four days! The whole club’s talking about it, you know, half of us are coming and the other half are pretending they don’t mind.” Susan had been at the club as long as the club had had women in it, and she conducted a conversation the way she played doubles, at the net, taking everything early. “Corran, I heard, the lawn over the chardonnay, oh, it’ll be heaven. And how’s Glen holding up? Fathers are useless at these things, aren’t they, they hold it together for months and then dissolve at the first bar of the processional. Tell him we’re all watching for it.”

“He’s rehearsing his toast like it’s a licensing exam.”

“Of course he is. Of course he is.” Susan laughed, and shifted the tart box, and for a half second something crossed her face that Carol read, in the moment, as sun in her eyes. “Well. He’s a lucky man, and I hope he knows it, and I’ve told him so myself.” She squeezed Carol’s wrist, ringed fingers warm from the box. “Saturday. We’ll be the table having too much fun, you know where to find us.”

“Table nine,” said Carol, who knew her own chart the way a pilot knows a panel, and Susan said table nine, that’s us, and went up the street with her tarts, calling hello to somebody else before she’d finished leaving. Carol walked home with the maps under her arm through the low gold light, past the church, past the hedge the town trimmed into battlements every June, thinking about nothing, or about ribbon, which was the same thing, and let herself in by the side door to a house that smelled of last night’s lasagna and this morning’s coffee and had roughly ninety minutes of quiet left in it.

Trish arrived at eleven, as forecast by Glen, in the elderly green Golf with the driver’s window that no longer sealed, so that they heard her before they saw her, the highway hum and then the radio and then the particular squawk of her parking brake, which she applied the way other people spiked a football. She came up the porch steps with a case of wine on her hip and a garment bag over her shoulder and her sunglasses pushed up into hair that had gone silver in exactly the way their mother’s had, which neither sister ever said aloud.

“Do not,” said Trish, by way of hello, “let me forget the second case in the trunk. It’s the good Cave Spring, I called ahead, I flirted with a child in a vest for it. Where’s my niece?”

“Getting her nails done with the bridesmaids. Hello to you too.”

“Hello, Care.” She set the case down on the porch boards, took Carol by the shoulders, and looked at her the way she had looked at her all their lives, an inspection disguised as affection, or possibly the reverse. “You look like a woman with a binder.”

“You have no idea.”

“Show me everything. I want the chart, I want the run sheet, I want to know who you’ve got me sitting with, and if you say the word cousins I’m sleeping in the car.”

She had her at table six with the Guelph contingent and two of Maren’s old teachers, a table Carol had assembled with the specific pride of a matchmaker, and Trish studied the wall in the dining room for a long minute, wine in hand at eleven fifteen in the morning because it was, she said, technically a special occasion for four consecutive days.

“You did the diagonal thing with Barb and Steve.”

“I did the diagonal thing.”

“You’re wasted on weddings. NATO should have you.” Trish tapped the empty seat at table nine. “Who’s this?”

“Hendersons’ son. Dropped out last week. Some conference he couldn’t move.”

“So there’s a spare seat at the table with, let’s see.” She leaned in, reading the little tented cards. “The club people, the club people, Dana Whitfield, the club people. God, Dana Whitfield. Is she still tall?”

“Still tall.”

“Some people have everything.” Trish moved on down the chart, and Carol straightened the empty card at nine, a small adjustment, a millimeter no one would ever notice, and made a note to ask Sylvie to pull the extra place setting so the table wouldn’t sit staring at a ghost.

They spent the afternoon on welcome bags at the dining table, the two of them and, from four o’clock, Lorraine, who arrived with Roger and, as predicted, a trunk. Local maps. Wine from a place Lorraine knew, better than the place Carol knew, according to Lorraine. Little bags of ice wine caramels that had to be counted into pouches. The three of them fell into a production line with the ease of women who had been assembling things at tables together for three decades, hand to hand to hand, while Roger and Glen stood in the yard around a barbecue that wasn’t lit, pointing at parts of it, agreeing. Lorraine narrated each item’s provenance at documentary length, Trish timed the caramel counts against her and announced splits, Carol quality-checked the tissue fans, and the talk ran the whole time alongside the hands without either interfering with the other: one more table, one more thing for one more occasion, which was, if you stood back far enough, most of what a family was.

“I said to Roger on the drive,” said Lorraine, counting caramels, “I said, mark me, this’ll be the wedding of the year. She’s been planning it since April, I said. Longer, knowing Carol.”

“Since the engagement,” Carol said. “So, fourteen months.”

“You see? I know my sister-in-law.” Lorraine held up a caramel like evidence. “These are from the place on the Parkway, the little one past the church, you know the one. Divine. I bought three extra bags for the car and Roger’s been into them since Grimsby.”

“One bag,” said Roger, through the screen door.

“Since Grimsby,” said Lorraine.

By six the bags stood ranked on the sideboard like a small army in cellophane, and Maren blew in with her nails done a color called Ballet Slipper, which was to Carol’s eye identical to her natural nails at twice the price, and hugged Trish for a full ten seconds, and stole four caramels, and stood in front of the seating chart with her ring hand flat against her chest the way she’d been holding it for fourteen months, as if the ring might make a break for it.

“Mum. It’s perfect.”

“It’s a draft,” said Carol, who knew it was perfect.

“It’s perfect, and I want to move exactly one person, and you’re going to say no.”

“Tell me at dinner.”

Dinner was fourteen people, leaves in the table, lasagna Carol had made and frozen in August against exactly this evening, and the noise of it filled the downstairs the way she liked best, Ben’s father telling a story about a bridge inspection gone wrong, Trish topping everyone up, Maren and Ben shoulder to shoulder at the far end laughing at something on his phone, and Glen at the head of the table catching Carol’s eye down the length of it once, in the middle of all of it, and raising his glass an inch off the cloth. To the general, he mouthed. Or maybe to the girl. It didn’t matter which. She raised hers back an inch, no more, their old semaphore, and let the noise close over the table again.

The lasagna went around twice, which was the correct number of times; she had frozen two pans in August against exactly this appetite. Glen served, because Glen always served, standing at the head with the lifter like a man dealing cards, calling down the table for plates, corner piece for Roger, no mushrooms this end for Ben’s sister, and Carol watched him run the table’s small logistics with the pleasure she always took in it, the two of them a kitchen with a wall knocked out, her production, his front of house.

Over the salad Maren tried to open negotiations on the seating chart, the one person, the move Carol was allegedly going to refuse, and Carol held up a hand.

“Chart office hours are nine to five.”

“It’s one person.”

“Nine to five. There’s a form.”

“There is not a form,” said Maren, delighted, appalled, and Trish said if there wasn’t a form yet there would be by morning, give her sister a laminator and a grievance and stand back, and the table went off down the track of Carol’s binder, its tabs, its rumored index, Glen testifying with his hand on his heart that he had seen the SUNDAY tab with his own eyes and that Sunday was only brunch, and Carol let them have it. It was cheap fuel and it was hers to give. A family roasting your competence was a family that had never once had to live without it.

Later, when the guest room light was out and the dishwasher was churning through its second load, she stood alone in the dining room with the lamp on, in front of the chart, and did her last pass of the day. A hundred and sixty-two names, minus one Henderson. She knew every one of them. That was the thing about a life like hers, built in one place, tended like a garden: you could paper a wall with the people who would come when your daughter got married, and there wasn’t a stranger among them.

She clicked off the lamp. Upstairs, Glen had fallen asleep with his book open on his chest, glasses still on, and she took the glasses off him the way she had ten thousand times, folded them onto the nightstand, marked his page with the flap, and stood a moment in the dark listening to the house be full.

Four more days. She had a tab for all of them.

That was the joke, later, the one she would not be able to tell anyone for a long time and would eventually be able to tell one person, at a kitchen table, in the rain: that on the Tuesday night she went to bed the most prepared mother of the bride in the peninsula’s recorded history, the binder fat with contingencies, weather and vendors and seating and shuttles, forty tabs deep into every catastrophe the world had ever thrown at a wedding, and the one contingency that was actually inbound, already in the air, already at the table in a lilac cardigan drinking the good riesling, had no tab, had never had a tab, was not a category the system knew, and the system was hers, and she had built it, and it was perfect, and it was aimed entirely the wrong way.

# Chapter 2

[T]{.dropcap}he plus-one arrived by text at seven forty in the morning, which Carol felt showed a certain courage.

Hi Mrs. H!! it read, from a number the phone identified as Roddy Cell. Quick one. is it cool if I bring someone Saturday. her name’s Jade. she’s super chill.

Carol set the phone face up on the island and looked at it for a moment the way she might look at a raccoon that had gotten into the garage. Roddy was Glen’s cousin’s boy, thirty-four, freshly divorced in the spring, and he had returned his RSVP card in June with the ONE box ticked, in pen, which as far as Carol was concerned had the standing of a signed contract.

“Problem?” Glen asked, buttering toast.

“Roddy wants to bring a Jade.”

“Good for Roddy.”

“There’s no seat for a Jade. There’s no meal for a Jade. The final count went in eleven days ago, the tables are set for eight, and Roddy is at your family’s table, which is full.”

“So tell him no. He’ll live. Roddy’s whole life is people telling him no slightly too late.”

“I can’t tell him no, because then Roddy arrives sad, and your aunt spends the reception telling everyone I turned away a young woman at the door like it’s a nativity play.” She pulled the binder over and flipped to the chart. There was the Henderson seat, open at table nine, marooned among the club people. She could move Roddy and the Jade to nine, except that split Roddy from his mother, who would mind, or move the Kellys, who would not mind but would notice, and noticing was minding with better manners.

Trish wandered in on the end of this, in yesterday’s shirt and sock feet, and poured coffee and read the chart over Carol’s shoulder like a bettor reading a racing form.

“Put the child and his Jade at nine,” she said. “The club people will adore it. Fresh meat. Susan Pell will have her entire biography by the salad.”

“His mother.”

“His mother raised him to tick ONE and then text you a Jade at dawn three days out. His mother can wave at him across the room.” Trish took a piece of Glen’s toast in passing, and Glen let her, and the ruling stood. Carol texted back, We’ll make it work. Jade is welcome. Chicken or fish for her? and got back three thumbs up and no answer, which meant chicken, which meant a call to the caterer, which she made at nine on the dot and had done in ninety seconds.

She redrew table nine on the wall after breakfast, pulling the tented cards and dealing them back out around a ninth chair, and paused with the last one in her hand. DANA WHITFIELD, in the serif that matched the invitations. She squared it on the cloth an inch from RODDY, then thought better of it, put the Jade between them, and stood back. Dana would be gracious about it. Dana was gracious about everything; it was her whole occupation now, as far as Carol could tell, grace and tennis and turning up. Twenty years of Boxing Days in this house, back when she was Dana-and-Colin, and then Colin was gone to Calgary with someone from his office, and the group had kept her the way you keep the good half of a broken set. She’d dropped off the last few years, her sister in Ottawa, something ongoing there, but she had sent the RSVP back inside a week, YES in a fountain pen, and a card to Maren with it. Lovely woman. Carol pressed the card flat and moved on to the shuttle list.

The shuttle was two runs of a twenty-four-seater between the hotels and Corran, and the driver, a retired school bus man named Wes, had already called twice to confirm things nobody else would have thought to confirm, the width of the winery lane for the turnaround, the exact wattage of his own courtesy light, whether any guests used mobility devices and how many and which hotel, questions that revealed a man who had spent thirty years delivering other people’s children safely through weather and had retired the vehicle but not the doctrine, which had endeared him to Carol permanently. She was on the phone with him about the second pickup, whether the Pillar and Post crowd would actually be standing outside at one fifteen or merely believe themselves capable of it, when Maren’s car pulled in behind Trish’s Golf and Maren came through the side door with two iced coffees and her sunglasses on her head.

“Office hours,” she said, handing one over. “I brought a bribe. Is the commissioner available.”

“The commissioner is always available to the bride.” Carol rang off with Wes, who was saying something about buffer time she already agreed with, and followed her daughter into the dining room, where Maren stood in front of the wall with her straw in her teeth, scanning, and then put her finger on table eleven.

“Steph. You have her with Ben’s work people.”

“Steph knows Ben’s work people.”

“Steph got me through fourth year and two breakups, and she’s coming alone. The invite said and guest, and she’s coming alone anyway, which you know took something, and I’m not having her spend my wedding making conference small talk with structural engineers. I want her at six.” Maren said it all in one breath, the case clearly rehearsed in the car, and stood braced. “You’re going to say the numbers don’t work.”

Carol looked at the wall. Six was Trish, the Guelph cousins, and Maren’s two favorite teachers from Eden High, a table she had tuned like an instrument. It was full. It was also, she saw at once, exactly where a woman coming alone should be, between Trish, who never let a glass or a silence sit empty, and Mrs. Feld, who had cried at the engagement announcement in full public view.

“Mr. Feld goes to eleven,” Carol said. “He’ll talk shop with the engineers all night and thank me for it, and his wife gets Steph, and Steph gets Trish.” She was already pulling the cards. “The numbers work.”

Maren stood there with her iced coffee. “That’s it? I had three more arguments.”

“Save them for your marriage.” Carol dealt Steph into table six and squared her, and beside her Maren was quiet a beat too long for the size of the transaction, and then put her head briefly on Carol’s shoulder, sideways, the way she used to over homework, sunglasses knocking Carol’s ear.

“Thanks, Mum.”

“It’s one card.”

“Yeah.” Upright again, bright again, checking her phone. “I have to run, we’re doing the license at two. Ben’s got the paperwork in a folder with a label on it, you’d love him.” And she was gone in a rattle of the side door, iced coffee abandoned a quarter full on the sideboard next to the ranked welcome bags, and Carol watched the car back out and thought that her daughter had been in the house eleven minutes, and that at some point in the last year eleven minutes had become the standard visit, and that this was what happened when children got jobs and fiancés and lives in Toronto. She filed it there, and the drawer took it.

The caterer’s office answered on the second ring, and the Jade adjustment took ninety seconds of business and then eleven minutes of pleasure, because the woman who ran the kitchen at Corran’s caterer was named Bev, and Bev had been feeding this town’s occasions for thirty years, and the two of them had history the way generals of allied armies have history. One chicken added, no charge at this count, don’t be silly. Then the real agenda: Bev wanted Carol to know, off the record, that the beef had come in beautiful, that the new pastry girl was a find, wait till Saturday, the little lemon things, and that whoever had negotiated the service timeline on this wedding, and Bev knew exactly who, had built the only run sheet all season that didn’t have the salads dying under heat lamps while somebody’s uncle finished a toast. “Mains after the father’s toast if he runs long,” Bev recited, with relish. “You put a fork in the road for me. Nobody puts a fork in the road.”

“I’ve heard him toast, Bev. I plan for weather I’ve seen.”

“You’re wasted on family,” said Bev, which was the trade’s highest decoration, and rang off to go shout at a delivery, and Carol updated the count sheet, 163 with the Jade, the Henderson plate already paid for, and stood for a moment enjoying the peculiar sweetness of a solved problem, the cleanest feeling her days ever produced, before the next line on the list produced the next problem, which was lunch.

Glen came home for it at noon, unscheduled, with two sandwiches from the good place on Mississagua Street, on the theory, he said, that the general had to be provisioned or the whole campaign failed, and they ate on the porch steps in the high golden middle of the day the way they had eaten on porch steps for thirty years, wax paper on their knees, Biscuit stationed at the exact center of the drop zone. Glen ran through the shop’s morning, the apprentice had found his keys and lost a ladder, an achievement Glen described with the wonder of a man watching a rare bird, and Carol ran the wedding’s morning back, Dave’s chairs, Bev’s beef, the Jade accommodation, and Glen listened to her logistics with the specific respect of one tradesman for another’s site.

“A hundred and sixty-three,” he said, when she got to the count. “You know what my dad would’ve said. That’s a hundred and sixty-three people who’ll know where the bar is by four o’clock.” He balled his wax paper and banked it off the porch post into the bin, a shot he’d been attempting since 1994 and made roughly annually, and looked so pleased that she laughed, and he looked more pleased at that than at the shot, and for a moment the two of them just sat in the sun on their own steps in the middle of their daughter’s wedding week, married, fed, sixty and fifty-eight, the whole enterprise humming, and Carol thought, with the plainness of a woman counting blessings the way she counted chairs: this. However loud Saturday gets, this is the actual thing. Remember you thought so on Wednesday, on the steps, over sandwiches.

She would remember. That was the trouble with her, as the whole town would have testified, admiringly: she remembered everything.

Glen came back from the shop at two with the truck’s windows down, and Carol met him in the front hall with a look he had been married to long enough to translate.

“The toast,” he said.

“The toast. Sylvie needs to know if you’re going over four minutes, because if you are, she brings the mains out after you instead of before, and she needs it by five today.”

“I’m not going over four minutes.”

“You’ve never in your life spoken for four minutes. Your retirement speech for Gord ran nineteen. There was an intermission.”

“Gord earned nineteen. Fine.” He rinsed his hands at the kitchen sink, disappeared into the den, and came back with three index cards and his reading glasses, and they set up in the living room, Carol on the chesterfield with her tea and Glen standing on the far side of the coffee table as if it were a head table, cards in hand, shoulders back, a big man in a golf shirt preparing to be heard.

“Should I do the voice?”

“Do the voice.”

He did the voice, the one he used for weddings and retirements and the one eulogy anyone had ever let him give, half a register down, slower, the voice of a man being listened to. “For everyone who doesn’t know me, I’m Glen, the father of the bride, which this week has mostly meant standing where Carol tells me and lifting what Carol points at.”

“They’ll laugh,” said Carol. “It’s true and they’ll laugh.”

“That’s the strategy.” He glanced at the second card. “Then the bit about the bike. Maren, age six, refusing the training wheels. Not asking me to take them off. Informing me. Standing in the driveway with the helmet already on, and I want it noted the helmet was on backwards, telling me, Dad, I’ve decided I’m going to fall a few times. And then she fell a few times. And then she rode.” He looked up over the glasses. “Too much?”

“It’s the best thing in it. Keep going.”

“Then Ben. First time she brought him here, and afterward everybody wanted my verdict, and all I could think was, he watched her the whole dinner. Not like a man waiting to talk. Like a man hoping she’d keep going.” He lowered the card. “Then I say something about the two of them, and then I say, Maren, your mother and I.” He stopped. “That’s where I keep stalling. Everything after your mother and I sounds like a fortune cookie. Your mother and I could not be prouder. Your mother and I wish you every happiness.”

“What do you actually want to say?”

Glen looked at the card, then at her, and the afternoon light was coming through the sheers behind him, and Biscuit sighed on the rug, and he said, “That she came from something. That the two of us built a thing and she grew up inside it and now she gets to build her own. Without it sounding like I’m taking credit for her.”

“Then say that. Not the fortune cookie. That.”

“There was a third story.” He shuffled to the back of the cards, where something had been written and struck through. “The lemonade stand. Summer she was nine. She ran it like a franchise, priced by cup size, kept a ledger in a duo-tang, and at the end of August she audited it and determined that her sole employee, who was me, had been drinking the inventory, and she docked me. Presented me with a written finding. I framed it, it’s still at the shop.” He looked at the struck-through lines with real regret. “Cut for time. You can’t do the bike and the lemonade, it turns into a roast.”

“Tell the lemonade one Sunday at brunch. Brunch runs itself, there’s room.”

“See, this is why you’re the general.” He penciled SUN, BRUNCH in the corner of the card, filing the story where his wife pointed, and Carol thought, not for the first time, that half of being married this long was serving as each other’s margins, the place the overflow got written so nothing was lost.

“Maren, you grew up inside something your mother and I built,” he tried, quarter speed, the voice gone, just Glen, “and the best day of my life is watching you start construction.” He winced. “Construction.”

“Leave construction. You’re a contractor, they’ll love it. Ben’s an engineer, he’ll love it.”

“You don’t think it’s corny?”

“It’s corny like you’re corny. It’ll land.”

He came around the coffee table and sat on the arm of the chesterfield beside her to pencil the line onto the card, his shoulder against hers, and when he’d got it down he stayed there a moment, reading his own three cards over, lips moving, and put his hand on her shoulder without looking, the weight of it entirely familiar, his thumb moving once over her collarbone, absent as breathing, a man touching the rail of his own stairs. Thirty-one years of that hand. She leaned into it a degree, the way you lean into weather you like, and read the cards along with him, and made him move the bike before Ben, and he was right about her being right.

He ran it once more, top to bottom, at performance volume, for an audience of one wife and one dog, the dog attending from the rug with the grave partiality he brought to all of Glen’s productions, and it was good. That was the plain professional finding from the chesterfield: it was very good, funny where it breathed and true where it counted, the work of a man who had loved being this family’s toastmaster for three decades and had saved his best material for the biggest room of his life, and when he finished, cards squared, glasses pushed up into his hair, looking at her over the coffee table for the verdict with the specific hopeful face he had been showing her across rooms since 1993, she gave it to him straight, because that was the office she held. “It’s the best one you’ve ever built. She’ll cry at the bike. Don’t look at me during it or I’ll go too, and one of us has to be photographable.” And he grinned like the boy who’d priced pipe-thawing door to door, and banked the note, and declared the cards officially ready for Saturday, and Carol drank her cooling tea in the lamplight and thought, not for the last time that week, that whatever else needed deciding in this life, the man could toast.

“Four minutes?” she said.

“Three fifty with applause.” He kissed her temple and stood, squaring the cards on the coffee table and patting his shirt pocket once out of habit, like a wallet. “What’s tonight?”

“Tonight Maren has her final fitting at six thirty and I’m going, and you are taking Roger, your sister, Ben’s parents, and your aunt to the Old Winery at seven, reservation under Hastings, and you are ordering wine that costs actual money, not the second cheapest.”

“Third cheapest.”

“Glen.”

“The Old Winery, seven, actual money.” He counted it off on his fingers. “And keep Lorraine off the topic of the federal government.”

“You can’t do everything. Just steer.”

The rest of the afternoon went to the shuttle schedule and a call from Sylvie about the terrace heaters, whether they’d want them for the receiving line if the evening ran cool, and Carol pulled up the forecast while they talked. Saturday: sun and cloud, twenty-one, wind off the lake in the afternoon, forty percent chance of showers overnight into Sunday. The overnight part was Sunday’s problem, and Sunday would run itself.

“Book the heaters,” she said. “If we don’t need them, they’re decor.”

“This is why you’re my easiest mother,” said Sylvie, and rang off, and Carol wrote HEATERS, BOOKED in the SATURDAY tab and permitted herself a moment at the kitchen window with the last of her tea. Out on the lawn, Trish and Maren were sitting in the two good Adirondack chairs with their faces to the sun, and whatever Trish was saying was making Maren laugh so hard she had both hands over her nose, and Biscuit was standing in front of them wagging at the sound the way he had wagged at that exact sound for twelve years.

Glen’s toast rehearsal cards were still on the coffee table where he’d forgotten them inside four minutes of writing them. She squared them, read the last one again, your mother and I built, and set them under his keys where he’d find them, and went upstairs to change for the fitting.

# Chapter 3

[T]{.dropcap}hey took Carol’s car to St. Catharines, because Trish’s Golf, in Trish’s own words, was not a vehicle you exposed a wedding dress to, it had opinions and one of them was dust. The drive out was twenty minutes of low sun, the vineyards running gold and green in their combed rows along the lakeshore road, nets on some of the blocks now against the starlings, and Trish rode with her window cracked and her arm out, narrating.

“Look at it. Honestly. You grew up in a postcard and you don’t even see it anymore.”

“I see it.”

“You see chair counts.” Trish put her hand out flat into the wind and let it plane, up, down, a thing she had done in the passenger seat since 1979. “You know what I remembered this morning? Mum, at your fitting. Nineteen ninety-five. She sat in the corner of that shop on Geneva Street with her purse on her knees like she was at the bank, and when you came out in the dress she didn’t say one word, she just took her glasses off. That was it. That was the whole review. Off came the glasses.”

“I remember.”

“Highest praise the woman had. She took her glasses off for you, for the twins’ christening, and for the Pope on television.” Trish pulled her arm in and rolled the window most of the way up. “You’ll be the one in the chair tonight. Strange, eh. One day you’re the dress, next thing you’re the chair.”

Carol drove a kilometer with that before answering, the vineyards feeding past, because her sister had put a whole cosmology in a sentence the way she sometimes did, carelessly, between window adjustments, and it deserved the kilometer. One day you’re the dress. She had been the dress for one afternoon in 1995, twenty-seven years old and certain of everything, and her mother had removed her glasses at her, and then she had spent three decades becoming the chair, the purse-on-knees, the corner of the shop, the review nobody performs and everybody awaits, and tonight her own daughter would come out of a change room and look first, before the mirror, before the groom’s opinion or her own, at the chair. That was the whole transaction, generation to generation, the dress turning into the chair turning the next dress loose. “It’s not strange,” she said finally. “It’s the only job I ever wanted. I just thought there’d be more warning between the two.”

“There’s never any warning,” said Trish, comfortable, prophetic, adjusting the visor. “That’s the whole racket,” and they came into St. Catharines with the sun going down the cross streets like a coin rolling.

“Is this you being comforting?”

“This is me being deep, and it’s wasted on you, drive.” But she was grinning, and she found the radio, the oldies station out of Buffalo that came in clean along the water, and they rode the rest of the way into the city with it on low, two sisters in a clean car with a garment bag of tissue paper riding flat in the back where the dress would ride home, past the hospital, past the old Zellers plaza that was something else now, the whole ordinary map of their lives, and Carol parked outside the shop with two minutes to spare, which Trish said was practically late, for her.

The bridal shop kept evening hours by appointment only, and at six thirty the front room was theirs: a wall of white on padded hangers, a dais in front of a triptych mirror, two velvet chairs, and a seamstress named Oksana who ran the room like an operating theater. Carol had one of the velvet chairs. Trish had claimed the other on the drive over, on the grounds that someone had to represent the common man.

Maren went into the change room with Oksana and the garment bag, and the two sisters sat with the flutes of prosecco the shop gave you whether you asked or not. Trish used the waiting the way she used all waiting, on inventory: the wall of white assessed dress by dress at connoisseur volume, the framed photographs of thirty years of Oksana’s brides, and one of them, near the register, identified with a small crow of triumph as the Feldman girl, 2004, whose marriage had outlasted every prediction made at her wedding. Carol drank her prosecco in the velvet chair and let her sister furnish the room with talk, the way an orchestra tunes before the piece so the silence after has something to arrive out of.

“Free bubbles at every fitting,” said Trish. “No wonder the dresses cost what they cost. You’re paying for a nightclub with pins in it.”

“Don’t tell her what it cost.”

“I know what it cost. I saw the invoice on the counter in your kitchen, because you leave everything on the counter in your kitchen. That number has a comma doing structural work.” She drank. “It’s a beautiful dress. I’d have gotten married a third time for that dress.”

“Please don’t.”

“For the dress, Care. Not the man. There’d be no man. Just me and the dress and open bar.”

The change room door opened, and Maren came out carefully, gathering handfuls of skirt, half turned to hear something Oksana was saying, and then she stepped up onto the dais and let the skirt fall and the mirror caught her in triplicate.

The dress had been beautiful in June. In June it had also been a size of an idea, pinned and chalked, a proposal. Now it was finished, and it fit the way things fit when a professional has measured your actual body four times, and Maren stood in the triple mirror in the shop’s yellow evening light, and Carol’s chest did something complicated and physical, a tightening under the sternum that she covered by setting down the flute with more care than it needed.

“Oh, kiddo,” said Trish softly.

“The bustle,” said Oksana, circling. “Watch. For dancing.” She dipped, gathered, hooked something invisible, and the train disappeared into a fold at the back like a magic trick, and she straightened it, and stood back with her hands folded, a woman presenting her work.

“It’s perfect,” Carol said. “Maren. It’s exactly right.”

Maren was looking at herself in the middle mirror. Not the way girls in movies looked, no welling up, no hands to the mouth. She was looking at herself the way you look at a document you’re about to sign, reading it top to bottom, and her jaw had the set it had had all week, and after a moment she said, “The buttons feel loose. At the wrist.”

Oksana came and took Maren’s wrist and examined the row of silk buttons, each the size of a pea, and pressed each one with her thumbnail. “Not loose.”

“They feel loose. If one goes Saturday.”

“They will not go.”

“But if one goes.”

“Then I have sewn forty years for nothing and I will move to the sea.” Oksana said it mildly, checking the last button. “I put a spare card of buttons and thread in the bag. For the mother. Always for the mother.” She glanced up at Maren’s face and then, briefly, at Carol’s, a professional reading a room she had read a thousand times, and moved around to fuss the hem.

“The veil now,” said Oksana, and went to the drawer where veils lived, and this part had a ceremony to it that she performed the same way, Carol suspected, for every bride, and that lost nothing for it. She shook the veil out once, let it hang to its full length, and carried it across the room over both forearms like something rescued from water. She set the comb, arranged the fall of it over Maren’s shoulders, and stepped back off the dais entirely, out of the mirror, leaving the bride alone in the glass three times over.

Nobody said anything for a moment. Even Trish, who could talk through a fire drill, held her prosecco still.

“Okay,” said Maren, to her own reflection, quietly, some negotiation concluding. “Okay.”

“Were you nervous?” she asked then, finding Carol in the mirror. “The week of. Actually nervous, or just busy.”

“Both. They braid together, you can’t always tell which strand you’re holding.” Carol turned her flute by the stem. “The Thursday before, I threw a shoe at your aunt.”

“You did not.”

“Overhand,” said Trish. “A wedge. She was a dead arm in those days, your mother. Ribbon dispute. I’ve told my chiropractor.”

“You have not told your chiropractor.”

“I’ve told everyone, Care. It’s my best material.” And Maren laughed on the dais, both hands coming up over her nose, the laugh that hadn’t changed since she was seven, and the veil shivered with it, and Oksana, back at her pin cushion, permitted herself the small nod of a woman whose room was performing to specification.

“Come see the back, Mum.”

Carol went up and stood behind her daughter, and the mirror held the two of them, and she checked the covered zipper and the hook and eye at the top of it, and the line of buttons down the spine, forty of them, and everything sat the way it should. Up close, Maren smelled of the nail salon and of her own perfume, the one she’d worn since university, and her shoulders under the lace had the particular rigidity Carol knew from science fairs and driving tests and the morning of her thesis defense.

“You’re allowed to love it,” Carol said quietly, to the mirror version of her.

“I do love it.”

“You’re looking at it like it’s going to be on the exam.”

“I’m being thorough. I wonder where I get that.” It came out with an edge that surprised all three of them, sharp enough that Oksana, at the hem, went diplomatically deaf. Maren closed her eyes for a second. “Sorry. I’m sorry, okay? I’ve been. It’s been a long week already and it’s Wednesday.”

“It’s fine.”

“It’s not, you’re doing everything and I’m snapping about buttons.” She said it fast, to the mirror, not turning around, the apology in the wrong shape the way her apologies had been in the wrong shape since she was nine, sanded flat and delivered sideways. Then she reached back with one hand, and Carol took it, and Maren squeezed once, hard, still not turning around, and let go and said, in a different voice, bright and bride-shaped, “Aunt Trish, come tell me honestly, is the bustle too much?”

“Honestly? Nothing about a wedding should be described as too much or we’d all have to go home.” Trish came up with her prosecco and the two of them fell into it, bustle up, bustle down, and the room refilled with ordinary noise, and Carol stepped off the dais and sat back down in the velvet chair and watched her daughter laugh with her sister and thought: nerves. Fourteen months of planning coming due, a hundred and sixty-two people, a new family bolting on. Everyone had a week where the wiring ran hot. She had had one herself in 1995; she had thrown a shoe at Trish over corsage ribbon, actually thrown it, a fact Trish had been dining out on for thirty-one years and would certainly serve again by Saturday.

Nerves. She filed it there, and the file took it, and if the drawer stuck a little going shut, she put that down to the prosecco and the hour.

While Maren changed out of the dress, Carol settled the balance at the front counter, quietly, the transaction she and Glen had decided on in July and told no one, and the shop’s owner ran the card and slid the folio across with the discretion of a bank, and when Maren emerged in her jeans with her hair loose and reached for her purse, Carol said the account was closed, and watched her daughter work out what that meant, and watched her open her mouth to protest the size of it, and watched her close it again, because some gifts you receive by shutting up, a skill Carol had spent years modeling.

“Mum.”

“It’s done. Say thank you and go say goodbye to your dress until Saturday.”

“Thank you,” said Maren, and got Carol’s shoulder with her own on the way past the counter, sideways, hard, the family dialect at full volume, and went to watch Oksana zip the bag.

At the door Oksana stopped them with one raised finger and issued her benediction, the same one, Carol suspected, she delivered at the end of every final fitting, and none the less true for it. “Saturday. Eat something at eleven, both of you, protein, I mean it, brides faint on champagne and air and then my buttons take the blame. Bustle after the first dance, not before, the photographs want the train. And when it is over,” she looked at Carol for this part, over the glasses, forty years of mothers inside that look, “the dress goes to the cleaner within the month, then into a box, and then, and this is the part nobody does, the box goes to her house. Not your house. Hers. The dress belongs to the marriage now.” She held the door for them. “Go. Sleep. Nobody sleeps. Go anyway.”

The fitting had run long, and the drive home ran quiet, the dress riding flat across the back seat like a sleeping passenger, Maren up front with her knees drawn up in the old way, Trish gone diplomatically quiet in the back beside the dress, and Maren watching her town go by in the dark, the school, the rink, the church where she’d been christened, the whole museum of herself scrolling past the glass, and somewhere by the old rink she said, to the window, “It’s weird. Everything looks like it’s already a memory. Does that happen to everyone the week of,” and Carol said it happened to everyone worth marrying, and Maren laughed, one note, and put her hand briefly over her mother’s on the gearshift, and neither of them said anything else the rest of the way, because the town went on scrolling and the dress went on sleeping and some drives are complete as driven.

They dropped Maren at the turn for Old Town, two taps of the horn as she went up the walk, gone to Ben. In the house on Prideaux Street the dinner crowd was still out, Glen steering Lorraine through a wine list somewhere over on the Parkway, and the downstairs had the humming, lamp-lit emptiness of a house between waves.

Carol hung the dress on the back of the door of Maren’s old room, where Trish was sleeping, unzipped the bag to let it breathe, and stood looking at it. Oksana had pressed it, but the drive had put a soft crease across the skirt where it had folded over the seat belt buckle, one line, faint, the kind of thing nobody but her would ever see.

The steamer lived in the hall closet. She filled it at the bathroom tap, waited for it to breathe, and worked the crease in long passes, one hand inside the skirt holding the fabric taut, the way she’d steamed Maren’s graduation gown and her own mother-of-the-bride dress the week before and, decades ago, her own veil, in a rented hall bathroom, while Trish held the mirror. The steam smelled faintly of hot cotton and nothing. Downstairs the furnace ticked on for the first time since spring; the nights had started meaning it.

Trish appeared in the doorway with two cups of tea and leaned on the frame, watching her work.

“She okay? Tonight she was a little.”

“A little. Nerves.” Carol moved down the skirt an inch at a time. “She scheduled her own wedding week like a shareholders’ meeting and now she’s living in it. She gets it from me, apparently. I’ve been told.”

“You did throw a shoe at me in 1995.”

“There it is.”

“A wedge heel. Overhand. I’ve told my chiropractor.” Trish came in and set Carol’s tea on the dresser and stood on the other side of the dress, holding the fabric out from the bag so the steam could get behind it, an old formation, the two of them either side of something white. For a while neither of them said anything, and the steamer hissed, and the crease let go all at once, as if it had only been waiting for attention.

“There,” said Trish. “Good as new. Nobody will ever know it survived a seat belt.”

Carol switched the steamer off and the room went quiet, and the dress hung between them, finished, breathing faintly on its hanger as the steam settled out of it.

“She’ll have a wonderful day,” Trish said. “You’ve built her a wonderful day.”

“Three more sleeps.” Carol zipped the bag to the waist, leaving the top open the way Oksana had instructed, and took her tea, and left her sister the room. On the landing she could hear a car slowing on Prideaux, voices, Lorraine’s laugh arriving before the front door even opened, and she went down already composing her face into the version that said, third cheapest, Glen, honestly.

The dinner party came in on a wave of night air and satisfaction, Lorraine first, unwinding a scarf, announcing to the house in general that the Old Winery had outdone itself, that Roger had eaten an appetizer he could not identify and asked for it again, that the sommelier, and here Roger, hanging coats, said the word sommelier with him, in unison, evidently the third telling since the parking lot.

“He was charming,” said Lorraine. “He was from Montreal.”

“He was from Welland,” said Roger.

“He had an accent.”

“He had a cold.”

Ben’s parents were behind them, glowing at half the wattage, and Ben’s mother caught Carol’s hand in both of hers in the front hall and said it had been lovely, all of it, and that Glen had been so generous, too generous, they’d tried to split it and he’d made the waiter an accomplice, hidden the machine, an ambush, and Carol said that sounded exactly like him, and meant it as fully as she had ever meant anything.

Glen came in last, carrying somebody’s forgotten cardigan and the smug ease of a host whose evening had gone to plan, and found her by the stairs.

“Before your sister files her report,” he said, “I want it on the record that I did not buy the third cheapest.”

“I heard. You hid the machine.”

“I bought,” he said, with the modesty of a man presenting a trophy, “the second most expensive. The Le Clos. The one we had at the anniversary.” He hung the cardigan on the newel post, pleased with himself down to his shoes. “Figured the week deserved it. One glass in, Ben’s dad toasted the two of us. Us, if you can believe it. I said save it for Saturday, and he said no, some things you say when you think of them.”

“And what did you say.”

“I said in that case, to Carol, who thought of everything.” He kissed her forehead, smelling of the good wine and the cold air off the street, and went to lock the back, calling goodnights down the hall, herding his sister up the stairs by remote encouragement, and Carol stood at the bottom with her cooling tea and listened to the house absorb them all, doors, taps, the guest room floorboard, until the landing light clicked off and it was just her and the hall lamp and the dress upstairs breathing on its hanger, and everything, everything, in its place.

The house shut itself down not long after in the old order, the dog’s last patrol of the yard conducted with ceremonial thoroughness and no findings, and the two of them went up the stairs of the house their daughter had grown up in, three nights before her wedding, in the fluency of three decades, his hand at the light switches, hers on the rail, and Carol lay down in the dark of the old world with the week’s whole beautiful machine idling ready in the driveway of Thursday, and slept the last untroubled sleep she would sleep for a season, eight hours, deep, unearned, the sleep of a woman whose ledgers, as far as she knew, all balanced.

# Chapter 4

[C]{.dropcap}orran Estate sat on Line 3 with its back to the river road, a long white house above forty acres of vines, and when Carol pulled in at ten on Thursday the crush pad was already running. Bins of chardonnay were coming up the drive on a flatbed, and the air through her open window smelled like cut fruit and diesel, the smell of the third week of September in this town her whole life. Two pickers waved her around the flatbed. Somewhere behind the winery a pump was thumping like a heart.

She had driven out the long way, along the river, because the morning had earned it, mist burning off the water in rags and the gulls working something out past the current, and the whole road was harvest: pickup trucks nosed into the vine rows, netting coming off the last blocks, a hand-lettered sign at somebody’s gate that said NO GRAPES TODAY ASK SATURDAY, which struck her as the most honest commerce she’d seen all week. She had grown up inside this calendar. School had smelled like this out its windows, September after September, the whole town leaning into its one frantic fortnight of bringing things in before the weather turned, and she had married into it and raised a daughter through it, and there was a rightness she didn’t examine too closely in the wedding landing here, now, in the bringing-in season, everything ripe and everyone working.

Sylvie met her on the ceremony lawn with a clipboard and sunglasses and a man Carol hadn’t met, introduced as Marcus, events manager, filling in this week while Paul’s on paternity. Carol shook his hand and clocked, in the way she clocked weather, that Marcus had the smile of a man planning to say no to something.

The checklist part of the walkthrough ran forty minutes and confirmed what fourteen months of planning had earned it the right to confirm: everything. The chair count staged and correct. The arbor anchored against the lake wind at all four feet, per the July addendum. The generator’s fuel logged, the accessible parking coned, the quartet’s corner shaded till three by the pavilion’s own shadow, which Carol had verified against a sun chart in February and which Sylvie had privately checked behind her and never mentioned, the two of them discovering the shared verification only now, at the corner itself, at ten fifteen, with identical satisfaction.

They walked the lawn first. The arbor was up, cedar, exactly where the June site visit had put it, with the vines falling away behind it to the east so that the two o’clock sun would sit behind the guests and not in anyone’s eyes. A hundred and sixty-two chairs would run eight rows deep with a center aisle, and Sylvie walked the aisle line with her arm out like a surveyor while Carol stood at the arbor looking back, seeing it peopled.

“Grass is dry,” said Sylvie. “They cut it Tuesday. If Saturday holds, it’s perfect underfoot.”

“And if Saturday doesn’t hold,” said Carol, “we’re in the pavilion, and the ceremony seating flips to the west wall like we walked in June.”

There was a small silence with Marcus in it.

“So, the pavilion,” Marcus said, consulting his own clipboard, easy, terribly friendly. “Just to flag it. With the forecast trending the way it is, if you want to guarantee the pavilion set for a ceremony flip, we’d usually look at a wet-weather standby fee. Crew has to hold Saturday morning to do the turn. It’s four fifty.”

“It’s in the contract,” Carol said.

“The pavilion’s in the contract for the reception, absolutely.”

“Clause eleven. Inclement weather contingency. The venue will accommodate relocation of the ceremony to the pavilion at no additional charge, decision by ten a.m. day of.” She didn’t have the contract with her. She didn’t need it; she had read it in March with a pencil, and the pencil was still in the margin of the copy in the binder on her kitchen island. “Paul initialed it. I initialed it. March eleventh.”

Marcus’s smile held, but it went to some effort. “I’d have to pull the file.”

“Pull the file,” said Carol, pleasantly. “We’ll wait. It’s a beautiful morning.”

It was. While Marcus walked up to the house, she and Sylvie stood at the arbor in the sun, and the flatbed came groaning back down the drive empty, and away to the north, over the lake they couldn’t see, gulls were riding something. Sylvie clicked her pen twice.

“For what it’s worth,” Sylvie said, not looking up from the clipboard, “Paul would’ve never.”

“Paul has a new baby. Marcus has a standby fee.” Carol watched the house. “Every trade has one Marcus. You just have to know where your paper is.”

Marcus came back down the lawn with a printed page and a changed posture, and said that yes, there it was, clause eleven, absolutely, his mistake, Paul ran his own amendments sometimes and they didn’t always make the master template, and the crew would be on standby Saturday morning at no charge, and could he just say the lawn was going to look incredible. Carol said he could, thanked him for checking so quickly, and shook his hand again, and his relief was so plain that she almost felt for him. Almost. The four hundred and fifty dollars stayed where it belonged, in her daughter’s honeymoon, and the morning proceeded.

The pavilion took an hour. It was a good room, timber-framed, glass down the whole vineyard side so that the vines ran floor to ceiling like wallpaper that happened to be real, and the morning sun came through it in long panels across the floor where the dance floor would go. Sylvie walked her through it at run-sheet speed, heels clicking on the concrete that would be under linen and votives by Saturday, conjuring the room with her pen. Head table along the glass, backs to the vines, so every photo of the toasts had the whole harvest in it. Band in the northeast corner, where the power was and the speakers could fire down the room’s length instead of into somebody’s soup. Bar at the far end, because a bar at the near end backed the room up at the door, a lesson every venue learned exactly once.

“Cake table here.” Sylvie planted a flag of masking tape on the floor a conspicuous distance from the dance floor’s edge. “Anchored. Weighted base, and it does not move, and no, I’ll tell you the whole story someday over something strong, but the short version is August, a conga line, and a groomsman named Chad, and we all lived, which is more than I can say for the cake.”

“How many tiers.”

“Then? Four. Now I have a policy.” She clicked the pen. “Yours is three, buttercream, it arrives at eleven and goes straight to refrigeration, and Chad is not invited, I checked your list personally.”

They walked the tables as taped. One through four down the glass side, family water, five through eight center, nine through twenty on the garden side, and Carol stood a moment on the tape square marked nine, mid-room, good sightline to the head table, a long diagonal to table one, and consulted the chart that lived behind her eyes. The club people. Susan Pell’s biography services. Roddy and his Jade. Dana. A fine table, a warm table, far enough from the band to hear itself talk. She had built it for the comfort of the people sitting at it, and she moved off the tape and on to the question of the gift table without a ripple, because on Thursday morning there was nothing there to ripple.

The heaters had arrived and stood along the terrace rail in a row like patient butlers. On the terrace itself Sylvie paced out the receiving line, door to garden gate.

“Bride and groom here. Then parents. Carol, you’re here.” Sylvie planted her on the flagstones two steps from the door, and Carol stood on the spot, facing the empty terrace, the way you stand where a photographer puts you. “Guests come through at maybe eight a minute if nobody’s chatty, and everybody’s chatty. Call it thirty minutes, hundred and sixty-two hands.”

“I’ve shaken this town’s hands before.”

“Then you know to leave the rings at home.” Sylvie made a note. “Flats behind the arbor. Water station at the gate end. If your feet die, there’s a chair and I’ll invent a reason it’s elegant.”

They finished with the rehearsal itself at four, the wedding party arriving in a ragged convoy, Maren in jeans with her hair in the trial updo from ten that morning, half collapsed on purpose so it wouldn’t peak early, Ben’s groomsmen carrying iced coffees, Ben’s mother in a sun hat she apologized for twice and should not have, it was a wonderful hat. With them came the flower girl, Ben’s niece Zuri, age four, who had been told what her job was and had improved upon it. Her father ran her training with a coach’s gravity and three official speeds: walking speed, church speed, and, after extensive negotiation, what Zuri termed her own speed, a demonstration of which cleared the aisle in four seconds flat and required the reset of two chair rows. Both families broke apart laughing, and the minister said, wiping his eyes, that they’d take any of the three on the day, the child had range.

The minister ran them through it twice. Processional order, who stood where, the handoff at the front, which Glen practiced with the gravity of a man rehearsing a structural weld, offering his arm to his daughter in jeans while the string quartet’s cellist, the only one who’d come to rehearsal, played from her phone speaker.

Between the two run-throughs the minister gathered the couple aside at the arbor, and the families, dismissed to the shade, could see but not hear it, the old man’s hands moving gently, Ben nodding, Maren’s head tipped, and afterward, walking back up the lawn beside Carol to reset the processional, the minister said, conversationally, that he told every couple the same three things at rehearsal and had for forty years, and would she like to know them, mothers always did. “One: on Saturday you’ll remember almost nothing, so pick one moment on purpose and decide to keep it. Two: something will go wrong, and it will become the story you tell, so be kind to it when it arrives. Three,” and he stopped to nudge a fallen chair marker straight with his shoe, “the vows aren’t for Saturday. Anybody can keep a vow at a wedding. They’re for some ordinary Tuesday years from now that neither of you can imagine yet. I tell them: memorize the Tuesday, not the wedding.” He smiled at the lawn, at the arbor, at forty years of lawns and arbors. “Nobody listens. The good ones remember later.”

At the top of the aisle, at the handoff, Maren kissed Glen’s cheek, and then turned and kissed Carol’s, off script, and said, “Both of you Saturday. I want to walk in with both of you,” and the minister said that was lovely and increasingly common, and made a note, and Glen looked over Maren’s head at Carol with his eyebrows up, and Carol nodded, and there it was, sorted, the way the best changes always came, at the last minute, from the bride, costing nothing.

The sun was low and the shadows of the vine rows had gone long and blue by the time everyone scattered to change for dinner. Carol drove the river road back into town with the window down. Boats on the river. The smell of somebody’s woodsmoke, first of the season. At home she showered and put on the green dress, the one Glen called the closer, and stood at the mirror putting her earrings in while Glen knotted a tie he’d un-knotted twice.

“Which one says my daughter’s getting married and I’m entirely relaxed about it?”

“The blue. That one says regional sales conference.”

He swapped it for the blue. In the mirror behind her he looked good, big and pressed and a little sunburned from the lawn, and he caught her eye in the glass and held it and said, “Two more sleeps. You did all this, you know.”

“Everyone did all this.”

“Everyone lifted what you pointed at.” He came and stood behind her and put both hands on her shoulders and looked at the two of them in the mirror, the closer and the blue tie, thirty-one years, and squeezed once. “Come on, general. They can’t start the dinner without us. I checked. I’m paying for it.”

They walked to dinner through the early evening, the whole family party on foot down Queen Street in the last of the light, because The Charlotte was three blocks from nothing and the town was made for exactly this. The procession collected commentary the whole way, shopkeepers locking up who knew half of them, a porch that produced congratulations, Lorraine narrating the historical plaques to Ben’s visiting relatives with a docent’s authority and roughly a docent’s accuracy. Carol walked at the rear of her own parade with Glen’s arm through hers while the streetlights came on ahead of them one by one, as though the town were lighting their way on purpose. The town had been lighting this family’s way for thirty years. What it cost was that the town saw everything, and she had always considered the price fair, and had two hours left of thinking so.

The Charlotte had given them the private room at the back, the one with the long table and the fireplace nobody needed yet, and by seven it was full: the wedding party, both sets of parents, the out-of-town aunts, twenty-six people at one table under warm light with the inn’s rosemary bread going around in baskets. Carol had the seating in her head, herself mid-table where she could reach both families, Glen anchoring the far end with Ben’s father, Lorraine within steering distance. Somebody had put a riesling from up the road in front of every second person, and Lorraine had found hers before her chair.

The room filled in eddies. Zuri was walked around the table by her father to say goodnight in pajamas under her coat, a celebrity appearance, and processed out one last time to applause. Ben stood to help the aunts with their chairs and got teased for it and kept doing it. The server, a young woman named Priya who ran the long table alone and made it look like choreography, took the wine order from Glen at the far end, and Carol watched the little conference, Glen’s finger on the list, Priya’s nod, and knew from the shape of it alone that actual money was being spent, per instructions.

The first courses landed and the table found its voice, twenty-six people redistributing themselves into conversation, the aunts annexing Ben’s grandmother for a summit on grandchildren, the groomsmen and the Guelph cousins establishing diplomatic relations at the far corner over the bread baskets, Lorraine and Ben’s mother discovering, to the visible delight of both, a shared jurisdiction over the raising of difficult boys. Carol ran her stretch of table on maternal autopilot, drawing out Ben’s quieter sister, resupplying the bread, conducting from mid-table with a question here and a redirect there the way she had conducted every family table since her mother had handed her the job, and underneath the conducting she did what she always did at the good tables, took the inventory: everyone fed, everyone lit, everyone talking to somebody, her daughter at the table’s heart with her hand in her fiancé’s on the linen in plain view, nothing anywhere in the warm crowded room requiring management, which was, for a woman of her construction, the feeling other people probably meant by the word peace.

The starters were the inn’s squash soup and a beet thing the kitchen was proud of. Ben’s father got the bridge inspection story up on its feet for the aunts, who were new territory, virgin soil, and told it better than he’d told it Tuesday, the pause before the punchline a full second longer, a man touring his material. Trish, from three seats down, caught Carol’s eye and mouthed, he’s added a raccoon, and there was, indeed, now a raccoon in the bridge story, and it killed.

Both families were braiding. The aunts had adopted Ben. Steph and the maid of honor had discovered a mutual grievance about Toronto rent and would be friends for life by Saturday. It was, by every instrument on her panel, a room in perfect trim.

Maren stood up before the mains, no glass tapping, just stood, and the table quieted the way a table does for a bride, and she thanked them for coming, and she was funny, she got Ben’s uncle’s name wrong on purpose and got the laugh, and then at the end, not funny, she said, “Saturday’s going to be big and loud and perfect, so I wanted to say the quiet part tonight. Every person at this table built some piece of me. That’s all. That’s the toast. You built me, and on Saturday you get to see what you built walk down an aisle in very expensive shoes.”

They drank to that. Carol drank to that, her eyes stinging in the good way, the way you budget for in a week like this, and the mains came out, and the room got loud, and it was, by any measure she knew, one of the best nights of her life. It would be important to remember that afterward. It was, right up until twenty past nine, one of the very best.

# Chapter 5

[D]{.dropcap}essert at The Charlotte was a lemon tart the size of a coaster and a plate of the inn’s shortbread for the table, and by nine the long room had reshuffled itself, chairs angled, jackets off, conversations running diagonally across the cloth. Ben’s father had the far end telling the bridge inspection story to a fresh audience. Maren had come down the table to sit on the arm of Ben’s mother’s chair, the two of them looking at something on her phone, the sun hat retired to a windowsill. Somebody had ordered ports.

Carol was pleasantly tired in the way of a general on the last night before a battle already won on paper. Her feet hurt in the closer’s shoes. The riesling had been good and she’d had two glasses of it over the evening, which for her was a spree. Beside her, Roger had gone contentedly quiet over his port, and Lorraine had rotated her chair a quarter turn toward Carol, settling in, her third glass poured.

Lorraine had been in reminiscence all through dessert, the deep water, back past the wedding to the boy her brother had been, and it was, Carol had to admit, good material. Glen at nine, going door to door in a February cold snap offering to thaw pipes with their father’s heat gun, a service he had invented, priced, and been forcibly retired from after one basement incident nobody would now fully describe. Glen at sixteen, rebuilding the neighbor’s furnace for free because the neighbor was a widow and it was December, and their mother finding out only in the spring, from the widow, at church. “He was always like that,” Lorraine said, watching him down the table with her chin in her hand. “Whatever was cold, he wanted to fix it. Mum used to say, that one will run himself ragged keeping other people’s houses warm.” She sighed the sigh of an older sister with sixty years of file on a man. “And look at him now. A whole company of it. Trucks with his name on them, all over town, keeping everybody’s houses warm.”

Across the table Ben’s mother had drifted into range, chair angled in, and offered her own boy at nine, who had taken the doorbell apart to learn it and reassembled it improved, chiming a note lower, a modification discovered only when winter guests kept remarking on how calm the house sounded, and the two women traded sons like gardeners trading cuttings, this one, here, take this one, and Carol listened and banked all of it, the doorbell, the heat gun, the widow’s furnace, thinking she would retell every piece for years, at anniversaries, to grandchildren someday, the archive doing what her archive did, acquiring, filing, keeping. That was the last clear pleasure of the old world, though she wouldn’t be able to date it until much later: sitting with her shoes off under a good table at a quarter to nine, adding to the collection, believing every item in it was what it appeared to be.

There had been a small unscheduled round of toasts with the dessert, the kind that happen at the end of good dinners when the scheduled speeches are all safely booked for Saturday and nobody’s on the clock. Ben’s mother had gone first, barely standing, one hand on the table, and said only that when Ben was small he used to ask her how you knew things, how do you know it’s going to rain, how do you know the bread’s done, and that she’d watched him across the summer knowing things about Maren without being told, when she was tired, when she was proud, when she wanted the window open, and that was her whole speech, that her son had finally found the thing he most wanted to know. It took forty seconds and put half the table into their napkins.

Trish had followed, because a room that emotional was, to Trish, a fire requiring immediate ventilation, and raised her port and said, “To the bar Saturday, which I’m told is open, and to my sister, who planned this entire week with a binder that has a tab for rain. If it rains, go find her and apologize.” Which got its laugh, and got a second laugh when Roger, gravely, said he had seen the tab. Ben’s father had risen to welcome the Hastings clan into the Okafor confederation with two sentences and a bow of real elegance. Roger, astonishing the entire table, had stood, said, “To the mothers. Everyone knows,” and sat down, five words, the toast of the evening by unanimous unspoken verdict, Lorraine staring at her husband as at a stranger of promising aspect. And Glen had risen last and declined to compete, glass up, “To Saturday. Long may we misremember it,” which got the laugh he’d priced it for and let the table back down into its noise. Priya ran the dessert service through the middle of all of it with a diplomat’s footwork, the lemon tarts landing like small municipal honors, the decaf question negotiated individually with each elder, and the fireplace ticking away on its dimmer for the look of the thing, and the evening had that quality Carol had spent fourteen months engineering toward and could now, from the inside, certify: a room in which every person present believed themselves to be at the good table.

There had been gifts, too, between the toasts and the ports, the couple’s thank-yous distributed down the table with Ben’s labeled efficiency: for the wedding party, engraved things in small boxes; for Ben’s parents, a framed photograph that made his mother deploy the sun hat’s pocket tissue supply; and for hers, a flat parcel Maren delivered in person, walking it down the table, that turned out to be the survey map of Crystal Beach from 1961, the year before her grandfather bought the lot, sourced from some archive by a bride with a planner’s soul, the family’s sand lane already drawn on it in a surveyor’s optimistic ink, and a small card that said, in Maren’s hand, FOR THE PEOPLE WHO GAVE ME EVERY SUMMER. Glen had put his reading glasses on to look at it and kept them on a long time. Carol had run her thumb once along the lane where the cottage would be, two years unbuilt, her father not yet arrived at his porch, everything still ahead of everyone, and had said thank you in a voice that required managing, and the table had moved on to the next box, and the map was at this moment on the windowsill beside the sun hat, wrapped again, waiting to go home and be framed.

The ports went around again on the strength of it. Priya moved down the table refilling coffees, and the room had arrived at that late-dinner equilibrium where nobody wanted to be the first coat.

Maren came down the table somewhere in there and dropped into a chair somebody had abandoned on Carol’s free side, hip first, the way she’d been landing in chairs beside her mother since she was four, and put her fork into Carol’s lemon tart without a word of negotiation, an ancient treaty, and the two of them shared the last of it in the noise while Maren ran tomorrow out loud, half to Carol and half to herself, nails at nine, the trial hair holding better than expected, whether the forecast was still behaving, and Carol confirmed the forecast from memory, chapter and verse, and Maren said, around the tart, “You know what Ben said in the car coming here? He said, your mum runs this town like a utility. Like the water. Nobody thinks about it and everything works because of it.” She licked the fork. “I told him you’d hate that and also that it’s the most romantic thing anyone’s ever said about you.”

“He’s not wrong about the hating.”

“You’re the water, Mum. Own it.” She knocked her shoulder into Carol’s, sideways, the family dialect, and looked down the table at everything at once, the aunts, the fireplace, her groom being made to arm-wrestle a groomsman for reasons lost to history, and for a second the bride’s whole guard came down and she was just a girl at a long table two nights before the biggest day of her life, sitting beside her mother, and she said, quietly, not performing it, “This is the good part, isn’t it. Right now. This is the part I’m supposed to remember.”

“This is the good part,” Carol said, and they sat in it together, shoulder to shoulder, while it lasted, which was eleven more minutes.
