Down the table, Glen was on his feet, not for a toast, just risen into a story, doing the wedding-and-retirement voice, telling the one about the furnace install at the monastery in 1998, where the abbot kept blessing the ductwork. He had the whole far end. Ben’s groomsmen were laughing before the punchlines, which Glen fed on visibly, slowing down, milking the abbot, adding a bishop this year, the story acquiring clergy by popular demand. Ben’s father, who had heard it Thursday at the rehearsal and Wednesday on the phone, was laughing hardest of all, the laugh of a man who has decided about another man and enjoys being right, and even the minister, down among the aunts, was wiping his eyes and disputing a doctrinal detail of the blessing of ductwork, which Glen incorporated mid-story. Carol watched from her end with her port and did what the whole table was doing, which was enjoy him, fully, for the last time such a thing would ever be uncomplicated.

The abbot reached his blessing. The far end detonated on schedule, Ben’s groomsmen pounding the table, Priya pausing mid-pour to laugh the professional’s rare unguarded laugh, and Glen sat down into the applause flushed and delighted, reaching for his wife’s eyes down the length of the table out of thirty years of habit, because the landing was never complete until she’d signed off on it. Carol raised her glass an inch off the cloth, message received, well flown, and he grinned like a boy and let the table have the rest of him.

“Look at him,” said Lorraine.

“He’s in his element.”

“He is. He so is.” Lorraine watched her brother with her chin propped on her hand, fond, a little glassy. “I was saying to Roger in the car coming down, weren’t we saying, Roger.” Roger, wisely, was examining his port. “We were saying, you’d hardly know it was the same man from five years back. He was gray, Carol. That stretch there, he was gray as a basement. And now look at him. The abbot.” She laughed at the abbot along with the far end, a beat behind.

Carol assumed the shop. The stretch after Gord retired sick and the arena contract went sideways, when Glen had come home with his neck in cement for the better part of a year. “The Gord years took it out of him,” she said. “Out of everyone.”

“Mm,” said Lorraine, in a tone Carol would spend a long time, later, taking apart. Agreement with a door in it. “Well. All of it. Everything at once, wasn’t it, for you two.” She patted Carol’s forearm, twice, her rings clicking. “Anyway, it’s behind you, that’s what tonight’s about. Look what you get on the other side. All this.”

Down the table, plates were being cleared, and the server leaned in between the sisters-in-law with the coffee pot, and Lorraine took cream and got sidetracked into the caramels from the place past the church, whether Carol had tried them with coffee, because with coffee they were a different experience entirely, and for a few minutes it was caramels, and the port went around again, and Maren’s laugh carried down from the other end, and Carol drank her coffee and felt the first cool draft of something she couldn’t have named move through the middle of the evening. Everything at once. She turned the phrase over once, found no place for it, and set it down.

The room had reached the hour when a rehearsal dinner stops being a meal and becomes a séance of the family’s whole history, and Lorraine was ferrying up treasures from the deep archive, their mother’s wartime economies, Glen’s first truck, the wedding of 1995 itself, while Carol half listened the way you monitor a familiar station. The third glass sat at Lorraine’s elbow at the top of its arc. She loved her brother too much and edited too little, and for thirty years of family tables the two facts had kept each other company without harm.

The evening had one more ordinary minute in it, and spent it on nothing: Priya clearing the last dessert plates, the fireplace ticking on its dimmer, Ben’s father and Glen negotiating who would settle the bill in the doomed courteous struggle both had planned for, Zuri’s father texting a photograph of a sleeping child to three tables at once, the whole warm apparatus running at cruising altitude with no instrument anywhere reading anything but fair skies, and Carol, in the middle of it, reached down and put her shoes back on under the table, the closer’s shoes, because the evening was entering its goodbyes and a general dresses for the exits, and that small act, the last thing she did in the old world, was the one she would return to all year: that she had put her shoes on to say goodnight, and the world had ended while she was doing the buckle.

It was twenty past nine when Trish, across the table, stood to go find the washroom, and the geometry of the table opened, and Lorraine leaned in on the vacancy with the confiding settle of a woman four glasses deep among family, and nodded down the table at Glen, who was now being made by the groomsmen to do the abbot’s voice one more time.

“I’ll tell you what I told him at Christmas that year,” Lorraine said, warm, unhurried, entirely kind. “I told him, you don’t deserve her, and I meant it as a compliment to you, dear, I said it right to his face in my kitchen. Because plenty of wives, and I said this too, plenty of wives would have taken the house and both cars and been right to. Not one Christmas in that whole business did you make it awkward for the rest of us, not one, with her sat right there at your own table every Boxing Day, bold as you like. I used to watch you pass Dana Whitfield the gravy and think, that is a lady. That is a lady, I’d think. I couldn’t have done it. I’d have worn the gravy, and so would she.”

The room went on. That was the thing. The room went entirely on, the abbot blessing the ductwork, the coffee pot traveling, shortbread crumbs being pressed up off the cloth with licked fingers at the far end, Ben’s arm going along the back of Maren’s chair, and inside all of it Carol sat with her cup halfway to her mouth and her face doing what she had trained it to do at funerals and retirement dinners and the reading of difficult wills, which was nothing at all.

Lorraine was still talking. It had moved somehow to Roger’s cholesterol. The sentence had simply continued on into the next thing, the way a car radio plays on across the moment of an accident, because for Lorraine there had been no moment; Lorraine had complimented her. Lorraine was choosing a shortbread now with the deliberation of a woman at a jeweler’s counter.

Carol set her cup down on its saucer without a sound. That took some doing; her hand had gone strange, far away, borrowed. She looked down the table at her husband. Glen had his arm out, being the abbot, sleeve pushed back, his face open and delighted and thirty years familiar, the face she had watched across every table of her adult life, and she watched the groomsmen laugh at him, and she watched Ben’s father laugh, wiping his eyes, and she watched her daughter, on the arm of the chair, laughing with her whole body at her father, and the room was warm and golden and full of everyone they knew.

Her own table. Every Boxing Day. Bold as you like.

The draft she had felt in the middle of the evening had found its name and its size, and it was not a draft, it was the door of the house standing open onto January, and it had been standing open for years, and everyone at the table had felt it except her, and they had all, every one of them, simply kept passing the gravy.

“You should have one of these before Roger inhales them,” Lorraine said, putting a shortbread on Carol’s saucer, tender as a nurse. “You’ve been running all week on fumes and coffee, I’ve watched you. Honestly. What would any of us do without you.”

“None taken,” said Carol, which was not the right answer to anything, and smiled, and the smile held, because it was a load-bearing smile, she had built it herself over thirty years and it did not go down easily. Down the table Glen finished the abbot for the last time and looked up the length of the cloth through the laughter, found her the way he always found her, and raised his glass an inch off the table. Their old semaphore.

She raised hers back. An inch. No more. Her hand did it from memory, the way a body finishes a stairway in the dark, and the port in the glass stood perfectly level, and she understood in a distant, administrative way that she was going to get every person in this room to their cars, and thank the server by name, and confirm the coffee urns for Sunday on the way out, and that she would do all of it inside this smile, because the alternative was to set the glass down and ask the question out loud, here, in the private room at The Charlotte, two days before the wedding, in front of her daughter and the shortbread and the sun hat on the windowsill.

Twenty-six people at the table. She began, without deciding to, to count the ones who would have known.

# Chapter 6

[S]{.dropcap}he got everyone to their cars.

She thanked the server, whose name was Priya, and confirmed the Sunday urns with the front desk on the way out, nine o’clock, two of them, one decaf. She stood on Queen Street in the cold that had come down with the dark and hugged Ben’s mother and admired the hat again and meant it. She walked home beside Glen with her arm through his and answered him in the right places, tired, he concluded, poor thing, out loud, patting her hand at the corner of King Street, you’ve been carrying this whole circus, and she watched the dark shops go by and said something about the urns. At home she hung the closer on its hanger and creamed off her makeup and lay in the dark on her side of the bed while Glen’s breathing went long and even in eleven minutes, a talent she had envied for three decades, and at midnight she was still lying there with her eyes open, running the ceiling’s dark the way she had run it through every crisis of the last thirty years, the practice’s audit of 2009, Maren’s fever winter, her mother’s last spring, all the nights this same ceiling had held whatever she’d put up there and given it back manageable by morning, and tonight the ceiling took nothing, tonight the machinery that had processed three decades of trouble met a piece of trouble machined from the same alloy as the marriage that housed it, and the piece would not go up, it stayed on her chest at its full installed weight, and beside her the man at the center of it breathed his eleven-minute sleep like a payslip, like proof of an order she was no longer sure had ever existed, and at half past she stopped pretending.

The stairs knew her weight. She came down in her robe without a light, past the ranked welcome bags on the sideboard, past the seating chart taped to the dining room wall, gray in the streetlight through the sheers, and Biscuit raised his head from his bed by the stove and considered the hour and came anyway, ticking across the kitchen tile to lean against her shins while she stood not turning on the light.

She turned it on. The little one, over the sink.

The binder lay on the island where it lived. She moved it to the counter by the fridge, out of the way, and got a pad of graph paper from the drawer where the batteries and the twist ties lived, and sat on the stool with a pencil, and did not write anything for a while.

The same man from five years back, Lorraine had said. That stretch there. She worked the arithmetic first because arithmetic was manageable. Five years back from this kitchen was the year of the kitchen itself: the renovation, the island she was sitting at, the good stove, Glen coming home from the shop with tile samples fanned like playing cards, courting her with grout. It had arrived out of nowhere, that project. She’d been saying new counters for a decade to a man who said next year the way other men said amen, and then one spring he was measuring, and by fall there was this room. And, later, Portugal for the thirtieth. The Douro at harvest, terraced vines to the waterline, Glen booking the good rooms without being asked, holding her hand on the boat like a teenager. She had told Trish it was like being married to a man who’d read a book about being married to her.

She wrote 5 on the graph paper. Then, after a moment: reno. Douro. New man.

Not one Christmas in that whole business. Whole business meant duration. Business meant years. She sat with the pencil and made herself do the other direction, backwards from five, and the kitchen was very quiet, the fridge cycling, the dog sighing against her feet, the furnace ticking in the walls like something being decided.

The Collingwood weekends. Twice a year, spring and fall, the supplier thing, Hastings Mechanical and every other mechanical outfit in the Golden Horseshoe at a resort learning about heat pumps, allegedly, golfing, definitely. Fifteen years of them, maybe more. She had never once wanted to go and had never once been asked to want to. That was nothing. That was ordinary; Susan Pell’s husband went to the same thing.

The phone. There had been an era of the phone face-down on the hall table, and then an era of the phone in his jacket in the closet, and then, somewhere in there, the phone had come back out and lived face-up on the counter charging like an honest appliance, and she could not, sitting here, put years to the eras, only that they had happened, the way you know it rained some summer without being able to say which. And she had never once found a second phone, which she had told herself meant nothing, and which she understood now, at this island, at this hour, could equally mean a second phone was simply never brought home.

That was the method the night kept teaching her, entry by entry: every fact she owned had a twin now, its innocent reading and its other one, standing side by side like the two columns of the graph pad, and the work was not choosing between them, the work was learning to hold both without the holding showing, because for three more days she was going to stand in rooms with people who lived entirely in the first column, her daughter’s wedding among them, and the second column was hers alone to carry, on a schedule, in bounded sessions, like everything else on the pad.

The eras kept coming once she’d opened the drawer of them. Maren’s convocation, June of 2019, inside: the three of them in the rented folding chairs on the Toronto lawn, Glen in the good suit filming the whole procession on his phone sideways, unusable, weeping, and dinner after at the Italian place where he’d toasted their daughter with a speech so unrehearsed and so perfect that strangers at the next table had applauded, and Carol had thought, that night, riding home down the dark highway with her shoes off, that she was the most completely lucky woman on the QEW. Inside. The anniversary cruise the year before that, the one he’d planned entire, inside. She made herself stop after the convocation, put the pencil flat on the pad, and instituted, at ten to two in the morning, the first rule of the new administration: the archive would be audited on a schedule, in daylight, in bounded sessions, or it would eat her. She had watched people be eaten. Not her. Not this month.

And the statement. She put the pencil down.

February of some year in the middle of it. She could see the envelope, the old Visa statements when they still came in envelopes, and she had opened it at the old counter, the laminate one, to check a charge from the garden center, and there had been a hotel on it. Toronto, the one by the convention center. A Tuesday, in a month with no conference in it and no reason on earth for a hotel room an hour and a half from home, and she had stood at the laminate counter with the page in her hand for a while. The furnace had been ticking then too.

And he had come home that night with butter tarts from the good bakery in Fonthill, and she had asked, not even asked, mentioned, statement came, what’s the Ashmont in February, and he’d said client dinner, the arena people, ran late, wasn’t worth driving back half asleep on the QEW, told you at the time, and he had put the kettle on and asked whether the garden center had had the paperwhites she wanted. And she had decided. That was the true word for it, she made herself look at the word, there in the graph paper’s little squares. Not believed. Decided. The way you decide not to reread a sentence in a letter, because the letter is long, and the evening is short, and the rest of it is in a hand you love.

She had never checked whether the arena account even existed in February. She was married to the man who kept the files. It had not occurred to her, and it had occurred to her completely, both at once, in a way she had not known a mind could do until she sat here at two in the morning feeling hers have been doing it for years.

Biscuit groaned and resettled on her feet. She wrote on the pad: Feb. the Ashmont. Butter tarts. She looked at butter tarts for a long time, and then she drew one line under it, not heavy, the kind of underline you make in a library book you don’t own.

The count from the table came back to her then, unbidden, the one she’d started at dessert and made herself abandon on the walk home. Lorraine knew, and had known long enough to make speeches in her kitchen at Christmas. Roger knew what Lorraine knew, minus retention. The club men, then, because Glen and Colin Whitfield had played in the same foursome for a decade before Colin left, and the foursome’s wives, because the foursome’s wives knew everything, they were the town’s aquifer. She sat very still and let the seating chart on the dining room wall assemble itself behind her eyes, table by table, in the serif that matched the invitations, and stopped herself at the edge of table one, where her daughter’s name was, because there was a wall there her mind walked up to and would not go over tonight, and she let it stand.

Dana had a trifle. That was the thing her mind produced next, with the terrible thoroughness of a flooded basement giving everything back at once. Twenty years of Boxing Days, and Dana brought the trifle in the cut glass bowl with the chip on the foot, sherry in it, don’t tell the children, and stood at the old laminate counter in this kitchen, in the before kitchen, whipping the cream by hand because Carol’s mixer frightened her, she said, a machine that committed. Dana dried the good glasses with a tea towel afterward, every year, sleeves pushed up, and put them away in the right cupboard without asking, because after enough years in a friend’s kitchen you know where the glasses live. Dana had known where everything in Carol’s kitchen lived. Carol sat at the island in the new kitchen and pressed her thumbnail into the graph paper and understood that she was going to have to take the whole archive down off its shelf, all twenty years, and read every ordinary afternoon in it twice, once as it had happened and once as it had actually happened, and that this, more than any single fact on the pad, was the size of the thing. The gravy was one dinner. The archive was her life.

And Colin. When Colin left, that January, gone to Calgary between one week and the next with the woman from his office, the whole group had closed around Dana like a hand. Casseroles, company, the men taking turns at her eavestroughs. Glen had gone over that same winter to fix her furnace, twice, the second time on a Sunday with the game on, because that’s what you did, that’s what Glen was, the man who came when your heat quit. Carol had sent him. She sat with that one for a while, both hands flat on the counter. She had sent him. She had made the sandwiches he took over in the truck. And she could not put a year to it, that was the vertigo of it, she could not remember if the furnace winter fell inside the whole business or before it began, whether she’d been dispatching kindness into a thing already underway or watering the ground it grew from, and the not knowing swung the whole decade loose from its hinges. Every act of decency in her marriage now came with a second column she couldn’t fill in.

Everything at once, Lorraine had said. For you two. The Gord years, she’d meant, the arena bid, Glen gray as a basement, and Carol had spent those years being the still point, holding the shop’s books some weekends, holding the man the rest of the time, and some of those Collingwood weekends had fallen in those exact years, and she had packed his bag for at least one of them. Folded his shirts. Put in the good razor. The pencil stayed where it was on the pad; there was no way to write that down that she could bear to read back.

And her mother. The pencil moved before she gave it permission, and wrote Mum, and stopped.

Her mother’s last year had been 2017. Fourteen months, the whole descent, and Carol had driven the QEW to the apartment in Grimsby three days a week and then five and then daily at the end, the pills sorted into their labeled days, the good flannel sheets brought over from the cottage because her mother wanted the cedar smell, the long quiet afternoons of a strong woman getting small. And Glen had been, and this was the entry she now had to make in the new ink, magnificent. Everyone had said so. He’d carried the practical whole of it, the wheelchair ramp built in one weekend, the apartment’s useless landlord handled in one phone call, dinner on the table those nights she came home hollowed out, never once impatient, never once anywhere but exactly where a husband should be, and her mother, who took her glasses off for almost no one, had said to Carol near the end, in the voice that spent words like a war ration, “You chose well. I wasn’t sure at first. I’m sure now.”

2017 fell inside. That was the arithmetic sitting in the little gray squares now, unblinking. Her mother’s dying year was one of the years, and every casserole and ramp and held evening of it had been performed by a man who was also, on some schedule she would now have to imagine or forbid herself from imagining, driving to Toronto, and she sat at the island with her hand flat over the word Mum and understood that this would be the entry that took longest, because her mother had died sure. Had died relieved about her, comforted about her, wrong about her. And there was no one left to tell, and nothing telling would fix, and the archive’s cruelest holding turned out not to be a Boxing Day or a butter tart but a dying woman’s settled mind.

Trish didn’t know. She tested it and it held, solid, weight-bearing: Trish, who applied a parking brake like a field goal and had never in fifty-four years successfully withheld a thought from her sister across a dinner table. Trish had read Dana’s name off the chart on Tuesday and said is she still tall. You could not counterfeit that. Somewhere in this flooded week there was one dry room, and her sister was standing in it, and Carol put her hand flat on the graph paper and breathed for a minute, in and out, at the discovery that this mattered more than nearly anything else on the pad.

What she did next she would have been unable to explain to anyone, which was to open the spice drawer. It had gone feral over the summer, everything walk-in, cumin behind the vanilla, two cinnamons, and she took it all out onto the counter in the small light and wiped the drawer and put everything back in alphabet, allspice to turmeric, one tin at a time, while her mind ran on ahead down the years without her. There was a comfort in it she did not examine. Somewhere around fennel she heard herself say, out loud, to the dog, “There was no tab for this,” and Biscuit’s tail beat the floor twice, agreeing, and the sound she made then was mostly a laugh. Mostly.

She did not cry at the island, and she did not go wake him, and both of these were decisions, made and remade in rotation, the way you keep a car on an icy road with a hundred small corrections. If she woke him it would be the middle of the night two days before the wedding and she would be the weeping woman in the robe demanding history, and he would be sleep-creased and gentle and would have the kettle on before she’d finished the first question, and she had seen tonight, at the table, watching him do the abbot, exactly how good he was in a warm room. She would not ask him anything until she was no longer guessing. She had run a dental office for thirty years; she knew what you did with a discrepancy. You did not confront the discrepancy. You pulled the file.

At ten to five the window over the sink went from black to charcoal. First birds. She made the coffee, the real pot, and while it dripped she tore the top sheet off the graph pad, folded it in quarters, and put it in her robe pocket, and squared the pad back in the drawer with the batteries and the twist ties. The kitchen looked like the kitchen. The spice drawer had taken her twenty minutes, somewhere in the deep of the night, an intermission her hands had called on their own authority while the rest of her regrouped, a small violent perfection imposed on four square feet of a world that had come apart at twenty past nine, and it had helped, that was the embarrassing fact, it had helped more than anything else the night had offered. The spice drawer closed on the first push, flush, alphabetical, and she stood with both hands on the counter he had built her, in the room he had built her, five years ago, when he was becoming new.

The first birds were properly up in the maple now with their usual excessive optimism, and she stood a moment longer in the graying kitchen and let herself feel the one thing the night’s bookkeeping had no column for: that the man asleep upstairs was still, at this hour, in his own understanding, a happy father a day from a wedding, and that some version of this week was going to have to be survived by every person she loved, one at a time, on a schedule she had just finished drafting at a kitchen island before dawn. General, he’d called her, for years, fondly, and she had taken it as a joke about seating charts. She looked at the closed drawer with the pad in it. Troop movements. She put the kettle on, because the day was coming whether she certified it or not.

Friday. Welcome bags to the hotels by ten. Trish would be down by eight, sock-footed, reaching for the good mug.

She would start with Trish.

# Chapter 7

[G]{.dropcap}len came down at seven fifteen, showered and whistling, and the whistling was the strangest thing Carol had witnessed in twelve hours of strange things.

He didn’t know she knew. She stood at the counter with her back to the room, rinsing a mug that was already clean, and listened to her husband move through his ordinary morning half a step behind her, the cupboard, the blue mug, the fridge door’s rubber kiss, the drawer where the bread knife lived, and understood that for him this was still Friday. Just Friday. The last full day before the wedding, a list of errands, a party tonight, his daughter down an aisle tomorrow. The house he was standing in still had all its walls. She had spent the night watching one of them come down, and he was buttering toast against it.

“Big day,” he said, to the room, happily. “Tuxes at two, right? I’ll come with, we can grab the chairs thing after.”

“I’ll drive,” she said, to the window.

“Even better.” He ate his toast standing up, the way he had eaten toast standing up since before Maren could walk, scrolling the shop’s messages with his thumb, reporting the overnight news in headlines, Peterson trying to un-move the arena meeting, the new apprentice locking his keys in the van again, second time, kid’s a genius with his hands and a menace with a fob. He laughed at his own telling of it. He put his plate in the dishwasher without being asked, a reformed man, five years reformed, and kissed the top of her head on his way to the door, and said, “Last quiet morning of our lives, general. Tomorrow we’re grandparent stock,” and was gone into the garage, and she stood holding the clean mug under the running water for a while after the door shut.

Grandparent stock. She turned the word over while the water ran warm across her knuckles. He had said it lightly, the way he said the true things, out ahead of both of them, and the word did what the words kept doing now, arrived at two depths, the surface one where a big man in a work jacket was happy about the future, and the other one, where a future was being assumed, jointly, out loud, a porch and grandchildren and the two of them in it, by a man who did not know that the joint account had been frozen at twenty past nine last night pending audit. She dried the mug and put it away, his chipped blue one, in its spot, because thirty-one years of muscle memory did not take instructions from new information, and stood a moment looking at it in the cupboard, the mug in its spot, everything in its spot, the whole kitchen a museum of an intact marriage, admission free, hours by appointment.

That was the man she had to sit across from in a parked car in seven hours. That one. The whistling one. She turned off the tap.

Trish came down at five to eight in sock feet and yesterday’s cardigan, took the good mug off its hook, and had it half filled before she registered her sister sitting at the island with the run sheet in front of her, dressed, made up, and visibly not reading it.

“You’re up early even for you.”

“Bags to the hotels by ten,” Carol said. “Three stops. I want company.”

“You want a valet.”

“I want company.”

Something in the second reading of it made Trish look at her properly, the mug paused, and whatever she saw she filed without comment, because upstairs the floorboard in the guest room had just announced Lorraine, and forty years of family had taught both sisters the acoustics of what could be said where. “Give me ten minutes,” Trish said. “I’m not delivering luxury goods in yesterday’s cardigan.”

Lorraine arrived in the kitchen in her housecoat as they were loading, full of intentions. She would come, she would help carry, many hands. Carol, without breaking stride, appointed her to the napkins instead, a stack of the good linen that Carol decided on the spot Sunday’s brunch could not do without, which wanted pressing and which nobody else could be trusted to press, and Lorraine received the assignment like a commission from the palace and went to find the iron, and Trish, wedging the last cellophane battalion into the back seat, murmured, “You’re wasted on weddings,” and they pulled out of the drive at twenty to nine into a morning of low white sky, the kind that couldn’t decide, the lake weather leaning on the town.

Carol took Mary Street, then the Parkway, the long way to the first hotel, past the golf course where the flags stood limp on their pins, and Trish let her, riding quietly with her window cracked an inch out of lifelong habit, waiting. That was the thing about her sister that the parking brake and the running commentary hid from strangers: she knew when to sit still. She had sat still beside Carol in a hospital corridor in 2003 and outside a principal’s office in 1986 and in this exact passenger seat, or its ancestors, on the worst three or four mornings of their shared lives, and she sat still now, watching the vines go by, until Carol pulled into the empty end of the golf course lot, where no one parked before ten, and put the car in park and left the engine running.

“Okay,” said Trish.

“I need to say something, and I need you to hear all of it before you react, because I’ve had one night with it and I can’t manage your reaction and mine at the same time. Yours will be bigger. It’ll use both lanes.”

“Care. You’re scaring me. Is it the girls, is somebody sick.”

“Nobody’s sick.” Carol looked out the windshield at the flat green and the white sky and heard herself say it in the voice she used for confirming appointments. “Last night at the dinner, Lorraine let something go. She thought I knew. Everyone thinks I knew.” Her hands were folded in her lap like a woman at church. “Glen had an affair with Dana Whitfield. For years. It ended, apparently, about five years ago. The family knew. The club knew. I found out at twenty past nine last night over a lemon tart, while Lorraine told me how gracious I’d been passing her the gravy.”

The car was very quiet. The heater breathed on their feet. Beside her, Trish had gone completely still, the stillness of a person rewinding a tape at speed, and Carol watched her sister’s face do in eight seconds what her own had been doing since midnight, the landing in stages, the no, the wait, the whole business, and then Trish said, “Dana Whitfield,” not as a question, and then, “Dana Whitfield who is coming to the wedding tomorrow,” and then she turned in her seat with her whole body the way she’d turned on people bothering Carol since 1979.

“Who knew.”

“Lorraine. Roger, so, Lorraine. The foursome, I’d guess, which is the club, which is the wives.” Carol kept her eyes on the golf course. “I spent last night doing the guest list. I stopped counting at a table and a half.”

“And me. Did you think, even for one minute, that I.”

“No.” It came out with a force that surprised them both. “No. That’s why you’re in this car. Tuesday you read her name off the chart and asked if she was still tall. You can’t fake that. Nobody alive can fake that, and certainly not you, you’ve never successfully hidden a Christmas present.” Carol’s voice did something on the last of it, a small crack, the first since the tart, and she pressed her lips together and mastered it. “You’re the one dry room in this entire town, Patricia. Do you understand what I’m telling you. Last night I sat in my kitchen and the only thing I knew for certain about my own life was that my sister didn’t know. I’ve been carrying that around since three in the morning like it’s a winning ticket.”

Trish looked at her for a long moment. Then she undid her seatbelt, because a seatbelt was not adequate to the situation, and took Carol’s folded hands out of her lap and held them in both of hers, hard, on the console between the seats, and said, “Right,” and “Okay,” and then, with the transition of a woman rolling up sleeves, “Tell me everything you know, in order, and then tell me what you need, and then, and I want to be transparent about my agenda here, we are going to discuss the gravy boat, because I am going to hit your sister-in-law with it.”

“You’re not hitting Lorraine.”

“A glancing blow.”

“She thought she was complimenting me. That’s the part I keep.” Carol looked down at their stacked hands. “She genuinely thought she was telling me I’d been magnificent.”

“They all think that,” Trish said, and there it was, arrived at in one stride, the thing Carol had circled all night. “That’s what this is, underneath. Twenty years of this town watching you and grading you on it. You’ve been getting a performance review for a play you didn’t know you were in.” Her grip tightened. “What did he say. When you.”

“I haven’t.”

“You haven’t.”

“He was asleep when I found out and building a wedding when I woke up. I’m not doing it in a hallway between vendor calls with his sister ironing napkins downstairs.” Carol pulled one hand free and put the car in reverse, because the first front desk expected them at nine fifteen and grief was not on the run sheet. “This afternoon. He thinks he’s collecting tuxes at two. I’ll drive.”

They delivered the bags. That was the part no one would believe later, she thought, and the part that felt, oddly, most like herself: the two of them carrying cellophane into three lobbies while the morning went on being a morning. At the first hotel the girl at the desk was new and overwhelmed and had the rooming list upside down, and Carol turned it right way up and walked her through it, this stack for the Okafor block, this for the Hastings block, the two loose ones held at the desk for late arrivals. The girl wrote it all down and said you’re so organized, are you with the planner, and Trish said she IS the planner, she’s the planner of everything, you have no idea, and the girl laughed without understanding.

By the car Trish had worked out the wording. “Everybody in three counties thinks they know you, Care, and every one of them knows a department.” She stood at the passenger door making her point across the roof. “The desk girl got logistics. The town gets the general. Glen got, whatever Glen decided to see. I’m the only living subscriber to the full edition, and as of this morning I’m not letting the subscription lapse again. We got lazy, you and me, we let the phone calls get short. That ends today.” She got in and buckled up like a woman sealing a treaty, and Carol pulled out of the lot with her full edition’s sole subscriber riding shotgun and found the morning, against every current running under it, almost bearable.

At the Pillar and Post the manager came out personally, a courtly man Carol had known across counters for twenty years, to say what a beautiful thing it was, three generations in the building for a hometown wedding, half his staff angling to work the Saturday shift just to see the decorated shuttle leave. Carol thanked him with the load-bearing smile and confirmed the one fifteen shuttle pickup, front portico, and watched herself do it from the small distance she’d been watching from since last night, the way she’d watched herself raise the glass. The smile held. That was the discovery of the morning. It held through the manager, held through the third lobby, held through a chance meeting at the doors with two of the Guelph cousins heading out to walk the golf course, held while Trish flanked her the whole way like a woman escorting a witness, glaring at bellhops on principle.

In between lobbies, in the car, they built the operational picture the way their mother had taught them to build anything, out loud, in pieces, passing it back and forth. Trish wanted the full timeline and got it: the tart, the sentence, the arithmetic at the island, the statement, the sandwiches. She swore at intervals, softly, with feeling, a rosary of it. Twice she reached over and gripped Carol’s forearm on the wheel, hard, and said nothing, and let go. Once, outside the second hotel, she sat with her hand on the door and didn’t open it and said, “The furnace. He fixed her furnace with your sandwiches in the truck,” in the voice of a woman reading the finding aloud to the court, and Carol said, “I know the file, Patricia. I wrote it,” and Trish nodded and got out and carried cellophane.

And once, between the second stop and the third, Trish went quiet in a different register, staring out at the golf course going by, and then said, “You know what I can’t get past? Me. I’m not making it about me, give me thirty seconds, it’s relevant.” She turned in the seat. “I’ve been divorced twice, Care. I have a nose for it. I can smell a marriage going off across a parking lot, it’s my one inheritance, I called the Pells’ rough patch in twenty-twelve from a Christmas card. And I sat at your table twenty times a year for two decades, across from him, across from her, and I got nothing. Not a flicker. And I’ve been sitting here doing the math on why, and the answer’s ugly, so I’m going to say it once and then we’re done with it. I didn’t smell it because I wasn’t checking. Your marriage was the one I didn’t audit. It was the control group. It was the thing I measured my wreckage against, the proof it could be done, and you don’t inspect the proof, Care, you just, you lean on it.” She faced the windshield again, jaw tight. “So that’s my confession and my apology in one, and it’s also why I’m going to be useless with objectivity this weekend and outstanding with trays, and now we never speak of it again unless you want to.”

“Noted,” said Carol, and reached over and gripped her sister’s forearm once, hard, the gesture returned up the chain of the morning, and Trish blinked fast at the windshield and said the third hotel had a revolving door and she had opinions about revolving doors, and they drove on, and the opinions were provided, unabridged, a filibuster of pure sisterly cover lasting most of a kilometer, revolving doors as a category, their history, their sinister economics, the incident at a Hamilton hotel in 1989 from which Trish had emerged, by her own account, a changed woman, and Carol drove and let the nonsense wash the car clean, which was its entire clinical purpose, and by the third lot both of them had their faces back on.

Back in the car after the last lobby, Trish did not reopen the conversation until Carol had pulled over at the river lookout on the Parkway, unplanned, both of them watching a laker slide downstream with its long rust flank catching what light there was. It took the ship a long time to pass the lookout, riding high and empty, bound down for the lake to load somewhere else, and the two of them watched the whole length of it go in silence, the way their father had taught them to watch ships, respectfully, to the stern, and the wake arrived at the bank below in its own time, long after the cause of it. She had been hit by the ship on Thursday night; the wake would be arriving for months, in lobbies and backyards and receiving lines, and the only plan was to stand where the water could move her without knocking her down. It was, as plans went, the best one she had ever made with less than a day’s notice.

“What do you need,” Trish said. “Actually. This weekend. Not the gravy boat answer.”

“I need Maren to have her wedding.” Carol watched the ship. “Whatever I decide, I’m not deciding it in the next forty-eight hours, and I’m not having her look back at her own photographs and see it. That’s the whole strategy. That’s all I have.”

“Then that’s the operation.” Trish nodded slowly, quartermaster to general, and Carol felt the plan acquire its second pair of hands. “I stay on you. Where you go, I go. Anybody corners you, I have a tray, I have an errand, I have a fake phone call, I once cleared a room in Guelph with a fake phone call, it’s a gift. And Sunday night, when that girl is back on the road to Toronto,” the laker’s horn sounded somewhere downstream, one long note across the water, “Sunday night, you and I open the good Cave Spring, and you fall apart properly, on a schedule, with a licensed professional. Me. I mean me.”

“I know you do.”

“Because you’re allowed, Care.” Trish’s voice went suddenly rough, and she looked out at the water until it steadied. “That’s the one thing I want said in this car. Whatever you do out there this weekend, that’s tactics, and I’ll salute. But you’re allowed. Somewhere in that binder there’d better be a tab that says so.”

Carol looked at the ship a while longer. Downstream the channel bent, and the long hull went out of sight in sections, stern last, unhurried, bound for the lake.

“Noted,” she said, which between the sisters was a signature on a document, and she checked her mirror, and pulled back out onto the Parkway, because it was ten forty in the morning, and the tuxes were ready at two.

# Chapter 8

[S]{.dropcap}he told Glen she’d drive to the tux place because she had to sign for the chair overage at the rental depot two doors down, which was true, and which she had arranged that morning to be true, moving the errand from Saturday with one phone call. He came out of the house at quarter to two in good spirits with his sunglasses on his head, folded himself into the passenger seat the way he had a thousand times, and spent the first ten minutes of the drive on the arena bid, which Peterson had tried to un-move from Monday, and which Glen had re-moved to Monday with some satisfaction. The apprentice had redeemed himself by lunchtime, diagnosing a boiler fault over the phone that had stumped two senior men, kid’s going to run the place someday if he can hang onto a set of keys. The party tonight: should he do the grill himself or let Ben’s father have it, there was a diplomacy question in there somewhere, he was leaning toward a co-grilling arrangement, a summit. He talked, easy, hands moving, a man riding shotgun through his own good life, and she drove and gave him the mm and the reasonable back, and behind her own eyes she went over her opening sentence one last time, testing the fasteners.

She had built it in the shower that morning and refined it across three hotel lobbies. Not a question. Questions gave him a doorway, and he was a man who could walk through a doorway carrying anything. Not an accusation with a verb in it he could dispute. A report. Delivered flat, the way you’d read a meter. 

Traffic thickened past the outlet mall and thinned again. He found the classic rock station and turned it down to talking level out of habit, courteous even to a conversation that didn’t exist yet.

The formal wear place shared a plaza with the rental depot, a mattress outlet, and a shawarma restaurant, and at two o’clock on the Friday afternoon the lot was half empty under the white sky. She pulled into a spot in the far rank, facing the fence and the drainage pond behind it, where a heron stood in the reeds pretending to be a stick, and turned the engine off, and did not reach for her door. The spot was chosen. Everything about the next hour was chosen, had been chosen at four in the morning with the graph pad open, because a conversation like this one needed a container and a container needed specifications: not the house, which held thirty-one years and a wedding’s worth of arriving witnesses; not a restaurant, with its waitresses and its acoustics, or anywhere with a table, because she had sat across desks from people for thirty years and knew what furniture did to the truth; not tonight, with the party on the lawn; a car, in a lot, in daylight, mid-errand, where the day’s machinery bracketed the hour on both sides and guaranteed the thing would have to be survivable, because at three they were due back in a wedding. She had built the container. What went into it now would still be entirely up to him.

Glen had his hand on his door. “You want me to grab the tuxes while you do the chairs, or.”

“Lorraine told me about Dana. Last night, at the dinner. She thought I knew.”

The hand came off the door.

She watched it land the way a statement lands, watched her husband of thirty-one years go still in the passenger seat with his sunglasses on his head and the shawarma sign reflected small in the lens of them, and she watched him not say what are you talking about, which was its own complete confession, delivered in under two seconds, before a word.

He took the sunglasses off his head slowly and folded the arms in and held them in his lap. Outside, the heron adjusted one leg.

“Okay,” he said. Quietly. To the fence.

“How long.”

“It ended five years ago.”

“That’s the other end. How long.”

A silence. She watched his jaw, the profile she had watched drive them to hospitals and airports and their daughter’s convocation, and she saw the exact moment he decided that the only road out was through, saw it settle on him like a coat.

“Twenty-sixteen,” he said. “Around then. It wasn’t. It didn’t start as.” He stopped himself, some draft of the sentence failing inspection, and went to the flattest thing instead. “Five years. About.”

“The Ashmont. February. The arena dinner that ran late.”

His eyes closed. That was all. A man being read the file. “Yes.”

“The butter tarts were fresh,” she said. “I’ve thought about that. You’d have had to stop in Fonthill on the way back from Toronto. It wasn’t even on your way. You added a stop.” She looked at the heron. “I’ve been doing that since two o’clock this morning. Taking things down off the shelf. It’s a long shelf, Glen. Twenty years of Boxing Days is on it. The furnace winter is on it. I made you sandwiches to take to her house.”

“Carol.”

“Don’t.” Not loud. It didn’t need loud. “You don’t get to say my name in that register today. That register’s for comforting me, and you’re not eligible.”

He absorbed that with his eyes on the fence, turning the sunglasses over once in his hands, and when he spoke again it was in a voice she’d only heard a handful of times in the marriage, at his father’s funeral, in a hospital corridor in 2003, the voice with everything taken out of it.

“It ended because I ended it. Five years ago, in the spring. I told her it was the worst thing I’d ever done and I was done doing it, and I came home, and I chose us. Every day since, I chose us. That part was real, Carol, the kitchen, Portugal, all of it, that was me trying to.” He stopped again. The sentence was headed somewhere he heard just in time.

“Trying to pay it down,” she said. “Without ever showing me the invoice.”

The heron took two slow steps in the reeds. Somewhere behind them a car door chunked, a family heading into the mattress place, a child’s voice rising and falling.

She worked the list the way she worked any reconciliation, line by line, no line skipped because it was painful, no line dwelt on because it was painful, the discipline identical in both directions. The Pells. Confirmed. The golf couples, most of them, probably all of them, he couldn’t say the borders of it, that was the nature of a tide, and she made him say the names he could say anyway, and he said them, seven of them, eight, each one a dinner table, each one a Christmas card, each one ten years of her own hospitality retroactively re-lit, and she took the names in without changing her face, entering them, because a ledger you flinched from keeping was a ledger somebody else got to keep for you, and she was done, as of twenty past nine last night, letting anyone else keep her books.

“Why didn’t you tell me,” she said. “Five years. You’d ended it, you were being the new man, you had every opening. Sunday mornings. Portugal. Thirty-one dinners a month. Why.”

And he turned and looked at her for the first time since the sentence had landed, and she saw that he had an answer, that he’d had it ready for five years the way she kept a spare button card, and he said, “Because it was over, and it was mine, and nobody was going to be helped by knowing. You’d have carried it every day for the rest of your life. I’d already made you carry it without knowing, I wasn’t going to make you carry it in the open too. I know how that sounds. I’ve had five years of knowing how it sounds. It was still true every morning.”

“It was convenient every morning.”

“It was both,” he said, and that, of everything, was the most honest sentence in the car, and they both knew it, and it sat between them on the console like a stone neither would touch.

She asked the other questions then, the ones from the graph paper, in order, because she would not get this stillness again, this sealed car with the truth finally in it, and she was not going to leave the room half inventoried. Did Dana know that she knew now. No. He hadn’t spoken to Dana beyond party talk in five years, hadn’t warned her, wouldn’t, there was nothing between them to warn. Did he love her. A long silence at that one, the heron holding its pose, and then, flatly, the worst answer because it was the true one: “For a while I thought I did. It turned out I loved not being at my own life. She was a place to not be at it. That’s uglier than loving her would’ve been. I know that.”

Who else knew. He went gray at that one, grayer, and did the accounting with his eyes on the fence: Lorraine had walked in on a phone call at the shop in twenty-sixteen and put it together, told Roger, promised silence, kept it in her fashion. Colin had known, or guessed, before Calgary; it might have been a spoke in that wheel, he’d never asked. The club, he didn’t know for certain, he’d never told a soul there, but the club knew things the way a beach knows the tide. He laid it out in the voice with everything removed, and she listened, and did not tell him about the sixty faces on the seating chart behind her eyes, and did not, though it pressed against her teeth the whole time, say their daughter’s name. That door stayed hers. He hadn’t earned even the knowledge of it.

The clock on the dash said 2:11. Inside the shop, four garment bags with their names pinned to them hung on a rail. Life had a nerve, she thought. It just kept scheduling things.

“Here’s what happens now,” Glen said, carefully, a man walking on a frozen pond listening to it. “Whatever you want. Whatever that means, the house, a lawyer, all of it, whatever you want, and I won’t say one word in my own defense, I forfeited that in twenty-sixteen. But Caro.” The old name. He heard himself use it and flinched and went on anyway. “Tomorrow. I’m asking you for tomorrow. Not for me. She’s been planning that day since she was nine, and you’ve built her a perfect one, and if it cracks open tonight it cracks open on her, in her photographs, in her marriage, at the start of it. Give me tomorrow. Give her tomorrow. Sunday night, Monday, the rest of my life, I’ll answer for every day of it, in any room, in front of anyone you want. Just not on her day.”

She looked at him. He was pale under the week’s sunburn, and his hands were quiet around the folded sunglasses, and he had, she noted from her small administrative distance, constructed the one request she could neither grant with any dignity nor refuse with any. He’d had since two seconds after her opening statement to build it, and he’d built it well, because that’s what he was, a man who came when your heat quit and fixed the immediate problem. The immediate problem was tomorrow. The furnace of the whole house could wait.

“You don’t get an answer in this car,” she said. “You get to sit with not having one, the way I sat with the whole thing last night while you slept. I’ll do what I decide to do, when I decide it, and it won’t be for you either way. Whatever happens tomorrow, you and I both need to be clear for the rest of our lives that it wasn’t for you.”

He nodded. He kept nodding a moment longer than the sentence needed, his eyes bright and fixed on the fence, and she realized he was working not to come apart in the passenger seat, and she sat with the strangeness of that, that some stubborn tidal part of her registered his distress and moved toward it after thirty-one years of moving toward it, and she let the impulse stand out there in the reeds with the heron, observed and unfed.

“The boys’ tuxes are under Okafor,” she said. “Ours is under Hastings. Check they gave you the navy tie, not the black, they mixed them at the fitting.”

He looked at her.

“We’re here,” she said. “The chairs still need signing. The heron doesn’t care, Glen. Nothing out there cares. That’s what I learned last night at my kitchen island. The wedding comes whether we deserve it or not, so go get the tuxes.”

He went. She watched him cross the lot in the flat light, a big man walking carefully, and go in under the bell on the shop door.

He could do it. That had never been in question. They could both do it; between them they could cater a hurricane. Through the glass she watched him summon, from wherever it lived, the abbot voice, the wedding face, and become in the eight steps to the counter the public man, shoulders up, hand out to the kid at the register, who said something bright and got a laugh back and would be telling his own family tonight about the nice big guy picking up wedding tuxes. The transformation took four seconds. She had watched him make it ten thousand times across three decades and had filed it, every time, under resilience. She sat in the car with her hands on the wheel and refiled it. She signed for a hundred and sixty-two chairs at the depot two doors down, initialed the damage waiver, took her yellow copy, and while she stood at the depot counter her phone went with the venue’s number, and she answered it, because that was the day she was having, and stood in the doorway of a rental depot forty feet from the car in which her marriage had just finished confessing, talking Sylvie through the final bar count, tonic, soda, the extra case of the sparkling for the toasts, her voice level and exact, and Sylvie said perfect, you’re a machine, and Carol said that she was, agreed a nine a.m. call for the standby crew, and hung up, and stood a moment with the phone in her hand in the smell of tarpaulin and cold steel, and then walked back across the lot, because the bar count was done and there was nothing else to hide inside.

She was back at the car before him, and when he came out with the garment bags over both arms she popped the trunk from the fob without a word. He laid the bags flat with a care that was almost unbearable to watch, smoothing them, squaring them, a man tending the one thing in the day that could still be handled correctly, and got in, and they drove home along the highway in a silence with the radio on low under it. The outlet mall went by. The escarpment ran along on the right, going gold at its top edge. Twice he drew breath to say something and released it unspent, and she let both breaths go by like exits not taken, and at the light at King Street his hand moved an inch on his knee, old instinct, headed for hers, and stopped, and went back, and she looked straight ahead at the light and let it be red for as long as it wanted.

The house received them at three with its arms full. Lorraine had finished the napkins and moved on, unassigned, to polishing the good flatware for Sunday, and met them in the front hall mid-task with a serving fork in one hand, wanting to know if the tuxes had come with the pocket squares or if the pocket squares were separate, because at Donna’s daughter’s wedding the pocket squares had been separate and it had been a scramble, a scramble, and Glen, setting the garment bags over the banister, said the squares were in with the tuxes, he’d checked, and his voice came out at party pitch, seamless, the host voice, and Lorraine sailed back to her flatware satisfied.

Carol hung the bags properly, one hook per bag, navy ties confirmed, and the two of them stood for a moment alone in the hall at the foot of the stairs, the afternoon’s freight between them and the house humming with family in every direction, no room in it, anywhere, that belonged to what had just happened in the car.

“Ice at six,” she said. “Ben’s bringing the speaker at five. You’re on the grill with his father, co-grilling, work out your summit.”

“Carol.”

“Party pitch,” she said quietly, already turning toward the kitchen, “from here to Sunday. You asked for the day. This is what the day costs,” and she went in to Lorraine and the flatware and the four o’clock list, and behind her she heard him stand in the hall a moment longer, and then climb the stairs, slowly, a man carrying two garment bags that had somehow become heavy, and overhead the shower ran a long time.

In the kitchen, Lorraine wanted opinions on the flatware’s arrangement and got them, and the four o’clock list stood ready on the island, and Carol worked it, item by item, her voice at party pitch, her hands entirely steady, and somewhere underneath the working, in the department that never closed, the afternoon filed itself: statement made, confession received, terms neither offered nor requested, three days of performance contracted at a plaza with a heron for a witness. The file would reopen Sunday at a scheduled hour. Until then there was a wedding, and the wedding, unlike the marriage, had a run sheet, and she had never once in her life failed to run a sheet.

# Chapter 9

[T]{.dropcap}he backyard on Prideaux Street had been assembling itself all afternoon into a party. The welcome do was Carol’s design from months back, deliberately the opposite of the wedding, no run sheet, no chart, folding tables under the maple, coolers on the porch, the good string lights that Roger was at this moment festooning between the shed and the eavestrough with the concentration of a man defusing something. Trish steadied the ladder and provided commentary, a running color broadcast covering Roger’s technique, the lights’ drape, the historical performance of this exact eavestrough at the graduation party of 2015, and the general standard of husbands on ladders she had known, a genre in which Roger, she announced, judged strictly on ladder deportment, ranked above both of her own, which Roger received at the top of the ladder with the grave satisfaction of a man being decorated. Zuri, delivered early by Ben’s sister, was helping, which meant relocating individual ice cubes from the cooler to the birdbath with tongs, an infrastructure project Biscuit supervised closely.

Ben arrived at five with the speaker, a bag of limes, and his particular quality of usefulness, which was to appear beside a task at the moment it became two-person. He held the ladder’s far leg for Roger’s final adjustment. He rescued the tong situation by giving Zuri a bucket with an official designation, ICE TRANSPORT AUXILIARY, lettered on masking tape in his engineer’s caps, along with a briefing on route and quota delivered at full seriousness down at four-year-old altitude, and Zuri, promoted, reformed on the spot from saboteur to service, and ran the auxiliary with a rigor that halved the birdbath’s losses inside ten minutes, and Biscuit, reassigned as escort, took up flanking duty on the new route, and the whole solved crisis stood as one more exhibit in the week’s growing file on Ben Okafor, a man who fixed things by giving their disorder a title and a lane. And he found Carol at the folding tables mid-list and worked beside her for a few minutes, setting out the good pens, squaring the guest book, quiet company, until he said, not looking up, in the level way he said everything, “I wanted to say thank you. Before it all goes loud. Not the toast version. Just, Maren keeps a screenshot of the first email you sent her about all this, back last summer, the one that said tell me the wedding you want and I’ll build the weather. She reads it when she’s stressed. I’ve seen her do it on the subway.” He aligned a pen with the table edge. “You built the weather. We both know it. That’s all.”

“Also, for the record,” he said, “she’s told me everything. The bike, the lemonade stand, the shoe your sister still bills her chiropractor for. I got the whole archive as a condition of the ring. I wanted you to know it landed somewhere safe.”

Carol looked at the guest book so the look on her face could be about the guest book. Two years she had known this young man, and he had chosen this among all evenings to be exactly who he was, and she thought, with a fierceness that surprised her, good, good, she picked one who watches, she picked one who says the quiet thing out loud on purpose, whatever else this family taught her she didn’t learn that from us.

“You’ll do,” she said, which in the family dialect was a decoration pinned on, and Ben, who had had two years to learn the dialect, inclined his head like a man knighted and went to help Roger down the ladder.

Maren arrived at five thirty with the card box, the guest book’s twin ribbons, and the particular density of a bride at T minus twenty-one hours, and she and Carol fell into the handoff at the folding tables, working down Maren’s phone list together. Card box on the gift table tomorrow, key taped inside the lid. Guest book here tonight, there tomorrow, with the good pens, not the ones that skip. The speaker was tonight’s music and Ben had made the playlist and Maren wanted it vetted, an audit conducted then and there at the folding table, phone between them, Maren scrolling, Carol ruling: the opening stretch approved, the aunts’ era generously represented, one song from Maren’s university years struck down on a technicality (“the lyrics, Maren, your grandmother-in-law reads lips”), the appeal denied, the replacement negotiated, and one entry near the bottom that stopped the scroll, the song from the basement on Lakeshore Road, which had no business on a twenty-nine-year-old’s fiancé’s playlist, and Maren, watching her mother’s face find it, said, “Aunt Trish consulted. She said if that one plays and you and she don’t dance to it, the wedding’s a failure regardless of everything else. It’s not for tonight, it’s flagged on the do-not-skip list we gave tomorrow’s DJ. Strategic depth, Ben calls it. When everyone’s defenses are down.” She reclaimed the phone. “You’re not the only one who plans, is all I’m saying.” The audit closed. It had passed, of course. It had been built to pass by people who had studied under her.

“Vetted for what.”

“Dad-danceable. If it’s too danceable he starts early and peaks at the wrong party.” Maren scrolled. “Also Steph texted me a paragraph about table six. An actual paragraph. She’s thrilled. Mrs. Feld already messaged her on Facebook, which, that’s a whole ecosystem, but she’s thrilled.” She said it lightly, but she bumped Carol’s shoulder with hers on the way past to the next table, a thank-you, and Carol received it and held very still around it, because since two o'clock that afternoon she had been carrying the car ride around the backyard like a full cup, and her daughter was standing eighteen inches away smelling of the same perfume she’d worn since university.

Glen was visible through the kitchen window, at the sink, washing the coolers’ worth of serving bowls nobody had asked him to wash, his back to the yard. He had been finding tasks like that since three o’clock. The two of them had run the afternoon in a flawless choreography of avoidance, passing information through logistics, the tuxes are hung, the ice is coming at six, and if anyone had noticed that the house’s two load-bearing walls were no longer speaking except in freight, no one had said so.

The thing went sideways over the schedule at the joint nobody was watching.

It was the timeline card for the morning, the one Carol had printed for the wedding party, hair at nine thirty, photographer at eleven, and Maren, reading it, stopped on the eleven o’clock line.

“You put first look photos at the house.”

“At eleven, before the family formals. That’s what we said in July.”

“We said maybe. Ben’s mum’s been asking about it, she wants to be there for his getting-ready photos at the rental, and if the photographer’s here at eleven she can’t be both places.” Maren rubbed her forehead with the back of her wrist. “It’s fine. It’s fine, I’ll just, I’ll tell her the schedule moved. I won’t get into the July thing, she doesn’t need the whole saga of who said maybe. Honestly, with the week she’s had with the hat, the less in her head the better. Nobody’s helped by knowing.”

Ben’s father arrived at six with folding chairs nobody needed and was received by Glen at the grill for what both households were already calling the summit, the two fathers standing before the cold grate with their arms crossed in the international posture of men about to have opinions about heat, and the negotiation that followed, zone allocation, lid doctrine, the correct hour to commit the sausages, ran twenty minutes and was conducted with the gravity of arms control, and concluded in a treaty so successful that each man spent the rest of the evening crediting the other’s concessions to anyone who would listen. Carol, resupplying the condiment table in their orbit, heard Ben’s father say, “Your Glen. He listens. You’d be surprised how rare,” and received it at both depths, the compliment true, the man it described real, the file it went into newly cross-referenced, and she said Glen had always been a listener, which was also true, and moved down the table with the mustards.

The yard went on. Roger achieved the eavestrough. An ice cube reached the birdbath. Somewhere down the block a lawnmower started up in the last light, somebody stealing a cut before the weekend, and Carol stood at the folding table with the timeline card in her hand and heard her husband’s sentence come out of her daughter’s mouth with its serial numbers still on it.

Nobody was going to be helped by knowing. Not a common construction. Not one you’d say cold. A phrase with a shape worn into it, the shape of being handed from one person to another in a kitchen or a car, a phrase that had done work in this family, that had been load-tested, and her daughter had just reached for it the way you reach for the scissors in the drawer where the scissors have always been.

“Mum?”

“Eleven’s fine to move,” Carol said. Her voice came out at exactly the right weight; some department of her was apparently handling calls independently now. “Tell Sylvie, not just the photographer, she keeps the master. Photographer on the boys at the rental for ten, and the first look at Corran, before the formals.”

“You’re sure? You had the light all planned for eleven.”

“The light will cope.”

Maren made the note in her phone with her thumbs, tongue between her teeth, twenty-nine and identical for a moment to her own homework self at the same table, and Carol watched her and did the arithmetic she had refused at the kitchen island at three in the morning, the wall her mind had walked up to and been permitted, that night, not to climb. Three years ago, give or take, something had changed in her daughter. The visits had shortened. The calls had become texts, the texts logistical. Christmas had developed a new choreography in which Maren was always mid-task, always adjacent to her father rather than across from him. Carol had put it down to Toronto, to the job, to Ben, to the ordinary continental drift of a grown child, and she had grieved it a little privately as such, and now she stood in her backyard while the string lights came on all at once to a small cheer from the ladder crew, and re-read three years in the new light the way she had re-read twenty at the island, and the sentence assembled itself with the same terrible administrative calm as all the others: she knew. She’s known for years. She learned it somewhere, from something, and she took it to her father, or he took it to her, and they built a phrase for it together, and then she came home for Christmas and sat across from me and passed the potatoes.

And the archive gave up its holdings, unasked, the way it had been doing for twenty hours. The Christmas before last, Maren in the kitchen drying glasses at the end of the night, and Carol saying, making conversation, shame Dana doesn’t come anymore, she always did the glasses, and her daughter’s shoulders, and the beat before her daughter said yeah, she was nice, and put the towel down and went to find Ben. The engagement dinner in Toronto, Glen reaching to top up Maren’s wine and Maren’s hand coming over the glass a half second early, reflex fast, and everyone laughing about the early flight in the morning. Her wedding dress appointments booked, all of them, every one, for weekdays, when her father was at the shop and could not possibly come along even in theory. Grains of sand, every one of them, nothing, and now they ran together into a shape, and the shape was a girl building a life with a security perimeter around it, and the perimeter had been for this, for the thing detonating, and it had held for three years, and it was going to fail this weekend through no fault of her engineering, because her aunt liked riesling.

Glen came out onto the porch with the dried bowls stacked in his arms, and Carol watched what she had never had cause to watch before, watched Maren clock her father’s arrival, the fractional recalibration, the way her daughter angled herself a few degrees toward the coolers, found the tongs, found Zuri, found a task, smooth as water finding a drain, and it was nothing, it was invisible, it was the kind of thing you could only see if you had been shown where to look, and Lorraine had shown her where to look at twenty past nine last night, and now she could see it everywhere, the whole family’s traffic pattern redrawn around a closed road nobody had ever marked on her map.

She kept watching, because once you could see a pattern you could not stop seeing it, and the pattern was a choreography two people had refined without ever rehearsing it out loud. Glen crossed the yard with the bowls, and Maren’s orbit adjusted; he stopped to inspect Roger’s string lights, and Maren drifted to the far tables; he called something to Ben about the grill, easy, paternal, and Maren answered for Ben, brightly, from a distance that never closed. Never a snub. That was the engineering of it, the three-year build: not one frame of it would read as coldness to a yard full of family, because it wasn’t coldness, it was distance management, load balancing, a daughter keeping herself out of rooms where the wiring ran hot, and doing it so fluently that her own mother had watched it across three Christmases and filed it under Toronto. Once, only once in the whole half hour, the choreography failed: Glen, passing the folding tables, put his hand briefly on his daughter’s shoulder, the most ordinary gesture in the family’s whole vocabulary, and Carol saw Maren receive it, saw her not flinch, saw her do something more expensive than flinching, which was to hold entirely still and let the hand exist and wait for it to lift, the way you wait out weather, and Glen moved on with the ice, having felt, Carol was almost certain, nothing, because he had spent five years perfecting the art of feeling nothing at close range, and her daughter went back to the tongs with her face perfectly pleasant and her shoulders one centimeter higher than before.

She did not go to her daughter and take the tongs out of her hand and ask her. She would want, later, to be able to say why not, and standing at the folding table she made herself write the reason properly, because it would have to hold for a long time: because whatever the girl had done with what she’d known, she had been a twenty-six-year-old handed her parents’ marriage in a box, and she had made some decision a daughter should never have had to make, alone, and had then carried it into her own engagement, her own wedding, this yard, tonight, and if there was an accounting coming between the two of them, and there was, it would not be run at T minus twenty-one hours, eighteen feet from her fiancé’s speaker, in front of the string lights. Carol had spent the day declining to detonate things on other people’s schedules. She could extend herself the same terms.

But it cost more than the car had. That was the fact she took into the pantry with her at six fifteen, carrying a stack of platters that did not need carrying, and set down, and stood in the dark among the tinned tomatoes with one hand over her mouth for the length of four long breaths. Glen was a wall of her house. Maren was the house. Somewhere out in the yard her daughter’s laugh went up, the real one, both hands over her nose, at something Trish had done with the tongs, and Carol stood in the pantry and let the sound go through her, love and loss in the same signal, no way to filter one from the other, and then she picked the platters back up, because the ice was due any minute, and went out.

The yard received her back without ceremony, which was the mercy of yards, and she re-entered the evening’s machinery at the exact station she’d left it, platters to the long table, and found that the four breaths had done what four breaths did, not fixed anything, nothing was getting fixed on a Friday, but re-seated the load, the way you stop on a stair landing and shift a box you cannot put down, and carry on carrying it, better, for a while. Trish tracked her across the grass with one glance, ran the full diagnostic in under a second, and elevated the tong comedy for Zuri’s benefit by one entire theatrical register, covering fire, and Carol set out the platters and squared their edges and took her place in her own backyard among every person she loved and one secret the size of the house, and hosted.

Trish intercepted her at the porch steps with a bag of limes and a once-over. “You good?”

“Recount at Sunday’s audit.”

“Copy that.” Trish took half the platters without being asked. Across the yard, Maren had Zuri on her hip and was letting her flip the switch for the string lights, off, on, off, on, to the child’s shrieking delight, the whole yard flashing like a signal to ships, and both sisters stopped to watch it, the bride under her own lights, laughing, luminous, twenty hours out.

“She has no idea you know she,” Trish said quietly, and stopped, because Carol had told her about the phrase in one sentence by the coolers twenty minutes before, sisters’ shorthand, and there was nothing to add.

“No.”

“Houseful of people,” Trish said, “all protecting each other in every possible direction. Like a junction box wired by a madman.” She hefted the platters. “Nobody touching anything in case the whole thing goes.”

“I’m touching it Sunday.”

“I know you are. I’ll hold the flashlight.” And she bumped Carol’s shoulder with hers, the same bump in the same dialect, mother and daughter and aunt all fluent in it, and went up the steps calling to Roger that the lights looked gorgeous and to leave them on, leave them on, the whole street should see.

Carol stayed in the yard a minute longer, alone at the folding tables in the string lights’ first evening, the party’s furniture standing ready around her like a set before curtain, and ran the day’s final reconciliation where she stood: the daughter accounted for, carrying what she carried by her own election, reasons written; the husband accounted for, at the grill now and tomorrow performing himself at full wattage; the sister briefed and holding; the sister-in-law oblivious and inbound; the town filtering into her yard within the hour with its ten years of knowing on its face. Every line item placed. Nothing balanced. That was the new accounting, she was learning its rules by the hour: a ledger where everything could be placed and nothing came to zero, and you ruled the line under it anyway and carried the difference forward, because the party started at seven thirty and the difference was yours to carry, that was the whole entry, forward, forward, forward.

# Chapter 10

[B]{.dropcap}y half past seven the yard was full and the evening had committed to being beautiful, one of the last warm ones, the air off the lake arriving in soft pushes that moved the string lights just enough to be worth stringing. Fifty people, maybe sixty, the out-of-towners and the close-by regulars, drinks in hand on the grass, Ben’s playlist running at vetted volume, the maple dropping a first leaf now and then into the proceedings like confetti from a very patient sky.

Carol worked the yard on rails of her own laying, a circuit from porch to coolers to the far tables and back that let her touch every conversation without being kept by any. She had dressed for it with more attention than the party warranted, the good linen, her hair done properly, and she was aware of the choice as she’d made it, aware it was armor, and had put it on anyway, on the theory that a general inspects troops in full uniform regardless of what the general slept.

Glen ran the grill end and the introductions, in his element and half a beat off it, and only two people alive could have seen the half beat. One of them was flanking her with a gin and tonic and a tray of deviled eggs she used like a cattle prod, steering conversations off Carol with offers of protein. The other was manning the grill.

The guests arrived in their streams. The Guelph cousins in a wave of hugs.
